<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:spanning tree}  
Given a connected graph $G$, a \textbf{spanning tree (ST)} of $G$ is a connected subgraph $T$ of $G$ without cycles, whose vertex set agrees with that of $G$. A \textbf{spanning-tree-decorated planar map (ST map)} is a triple $(M,\vv{e},T)$ where $M$ is a planar map, $\vv{e}$ is a directed edge of $M$, and $T$ is an ST of $M$. The directed edge $\vv{e}$ is called the \textbf{root edge}, and its terminal vertex $r$ is called the \textbf{root vertex}. 

Every ST map $(M,\vv{e},T)$ is associated with a dual ST map $(M^{*},\vv{e}^{*},T^{*})$ such that $M^{*}$ is the dual map of $M$, $\vv{e}^{*}$ is the directed edge of $M^{*}$ that crosses $\vv{e}$ from right to left (with respect to the orientation of $\vv{e}$), and $T^{*}$ consists of the edges in $M^{*}$ whose duals are not in $T$. The directed edge $\vv{e}^{*}$ is called the \textbf{dual root edge}, its terminal vertex $r^{*}$ is called the \textbf{dual terminal vertex}, and $T^{*}$ is called the \textbf{dual spanning tree (dual ST)}.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:orientation on ST map}
For an arbitrary ST map $(M,\vv{e},T)$, we associate it with an orientation $\vv{E}=\vv{E}(M,\vv{e},T)$ in the following way: 
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
    \item Every edge in $T$ is doubly oriented.\footnote{Intuitively, this is because the contour exploration of $T$ visits every edge in $T$ twice and in the opposite order.}
    \item Every edge not in $T$ is singly oriented from the first  contour exploration  crossing point to the second  contour exploration  crossing point. (See \Cref{fig:directed ST map} for an illustration.) Let $\vv*{\mathcal{E}}{T^{c}}\subset \vv{\mathcal{E}}(M)\setminus \vv{\mathcal{E}}(T)$ be the set of these directed edges.
\end{itemize}
We let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:set of directed edge}
\vv{E}_{M}:=\vv{\mathcal{E}}(T)\cup\vv*{\mathcal{E}}{T^{c}}.
\end{equation}
The quadruple $(M,\vv{e},T,\vv{E}_{M})$ is called the \textbf{directed spanning-tree-decorated planar map (directed ST map)}.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:directed distance}
A (finite or infinite) \textbf{directed path} $P$ on a directed planar map $(M,\vv{E})$ is a function from $\big[1,|P|\big]\cap\mathbb{Z}$ to $\vv{E}$, where $|P|\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}$ is called the \textbf{length} of $P$, such that for every $k\in\big[2,|P|\big]\cap\mathbb{Z}$, the initial vertex of $P(k)$ is the same as the terminal vertex of $P(k-1)$. A directed path of length $0$ is just a single vertex.

For all $x,x'\in\mathcal{V}(M)$, we write $\mathrm{SDP}_{(M,\vv{E})}(x,x')$ for the \textbf{length of the shortest directed path} in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $x'$, if at least one such path exists. If there is no directed path from $x$ to $x'$, we write $\mathrm{SDP}_{(M,\vv{E})}(x,x')=\infty$. When the orientation is clear from the context, we just write $\mathrm{SDP}_{M}$ instead of $\mathrm{SDP}_{(M,\vv{E})}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:FUIST map}
The \textbf{future uniform infinite spanning-tree-decorated planar map (FUIST map)}, denoted by $(M_{0,\infty},\lambda_{0},T_{0,\infty})$, is the submap of the UIST map $(M_{-\infty,\infty},T_{-\infty,\infty})$ consisting of the vertices and edges in $(M_{-\infty,\infty},T_{-\infty,\infty})$ that lie weakly to the left of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}\cup\{\lambda_{0}\}\cup\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, where left is with respect to the orientation of $\lambda_0$ that goes from $x_{0}$ to $y_{0}$. Edges of $M_{-\infty,\infty}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ are included. Edges of $M_{-\infty,\infty}$ that cross $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ are not included (see the black dashed edges in \Cref{fig:fuist}). The restriction of $T_{-\infty,\infty}$ to the above submap gives the connected infinite ST $T_{0,\infty}$ on $M_{0,\infty}$.

The orientation $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ on $(M_{0,\infty},\lambda_{0},T_{0,\infty})$ is inherited from the orientation $\vv{E}_{-\infty,\infty}$ on $M_{-\infty,\infty}$, except that every edge on the infinite branch $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ in $T_{0,\infty}$ is singly oriented from $x_{-(k-1)}$ to $x_{-k}$ for every $k\geq 1$. The quadruple $(M_{0,\infty},\lambda_{0},T_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ is called the \textbf{directed future uniform infinite spanning-tree-decorated planar map (directed FUIST map)}.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:Busemann exists}
Almost surely, there exists a unique function $\mathcal{X}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}$,
called the \textbf{Busemann function} on the directed FUIST map, such that $\mathcal{X}(0)$ is $0$ and the following holds. Let $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$ and
let $\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}$ be an arbitrary sequence of distinct vertices of $M_{0,\infty}$ such that, for each $j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, each of $x_{k}$ and $x_{k'}$ can be joined to $w_{j}$ by a directed path in $(M_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$.\footnote{The existence of such a sequence is explained as follows. We know that $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, viewed as an infinite directed path started from $x_{0}$, is an infinite directed path in $(M_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. We also know that every edge of the infinite ST $T_{0,\infty}$ on $M_{0,\infty}$ not belonging to $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ is doubly oriented. As a result, for every vertex $u\in M_{0,\infty}$, the unique infinite branch in $T_{0,\infty}$ from $u$ to $\infty$ is an infinite directed path in $(M_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. Since $T_{0,\infty}$ is a connected infinite ST, for all $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$, the two infinite branches in $T_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{k}$ and $x_{k'}$ to $\infty$ must merge with $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ at some points. Thus we can simply take $\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}$ as $\{x_{-K-j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}$ for some large enough $K=K(k,k')\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$.} Then for each sufficiently large $j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann exists}
\mathcal{X}(k')-\mathcal{X}(k)=\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k'},w_{j})-\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j}).    
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:scaling limit}
Under the local Skorokhod topology on $\mathbb{R}$, as $k\to\infty$, the re-scaled Busemann function $\big\{k^{-2/3}\mathcal{X}(\lfloor kt\rfloor)\big\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}$ converges in law to a stochastic process $X$ from $\mathbb{R}$ to $\mathbb{R}$ such that $X|_{[0,\infty)}$ and $X(-\cdot)|_{[0,\infty)}$ are i.i.d.~strictly stable mean zero $3/2$-stable L\'evy processes with only upward jumps.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:properties of Busemann}
The following properties hold for the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*),leftmargin=*]
\item\label{property:independence}\textbf{(Independent increments).} The increments $\{\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ are independent.
\item\label{property:stationarity}\textbf{(Stationary increments).} The increments $\{(\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)\}_{k\geq 1}$ have the same distribution, and the increments $\{(\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)\}_{k\leq 0}$ have the same distribution.
\item\label{property:exponential negative tail for positive}\textbf{(Exponential negative tail for $k\geq 1$).} The common distribution of the increments $\{\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)\}_{k\geq 1}$ has an exponential tail on its negative part. That is, there is a positive constant $c_{1}>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exponential tail for positive}
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\leq -x]\leq \exp(-c_{1} x), \quad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.  
\end{equation}
\item\label{property:bound for negative}\textbf{(Deterministic bounds for $k\leq 0$).} We have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:deterministic bounds for negative}
\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)\leq 1, \quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}.     
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:tail estimate}
Both $\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)$ have mean $0$. In addition, there is a positive constant $c_{2}>0$ such that as $x\to\infty$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tail estimate positive}
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\geq x] = c_{2} x^{-3/2}+o(x^{-3/2}),  
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tail estimate negative}
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\leq -x] = c_{2} x^{-3/2}+o(x^{-3/2}).     
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Follows directly from the heavy-tailed central limit theorem (see e.g.~\cite[Theorems 4.5.1-4.5.3]{Whi02}).
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:uniform meandric system}
A \textbf{meandric system} of size $n$, denoted by $\mathit{MS}_{n}$, is a pair consisting of an upper arc diagram and a lower arc diagram, both of size $n$. We interpret $\mathit{MS}_{n}$ as a planar map whose vertices are the points $1,2,\dots,2n$ and whose edges consist of (1) the segments $[i,i+1],1\leq i\leq 2n-1,$ on $\mathbb{R}$; (2) all the arcs in the upper arc diagram; and (3) all the arcs in the lower arc diagram. 

For each meandric system $\mathit{MS}_{n}$, its associated orientation $\vv{E}^{\mathit{MS}}_{n}$ consists of all the directed edges in $\vv{\mathcal{E}}(\mathit{MS}_{n})$ that go from the point $i$ to the point $j$ with $i<j$. The pair $(\mathit{MS}_{n},\vv{E}^{\mathit{MS}}_{n})$ is called a \textbf{directed meandric system} of size $n$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:bound ms}
For each $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, let $(\mathit{MS}_{n},\vv{E}^{\mathit{MS}}_{n})$ be the directed uniform meandric system of size $n$. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant upper and lower bound ms}
\mathbb{P}\Big[n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-5}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathit{MS}_{n}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{8}\Big] = 1-O_{n}(n^{-p}),\quad\text{for every }p>0. 
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:mated-CRT}
Let $Z=(L,R)$, where $L$ and $R$ are two independent bi-infinite one-dimensional Brownian motions with $L(0)=R(0)=0$. The \textbf{infinite $\sqrt{2}$-mated-CRT map} is a planar map $\mathcal{G}_{-\infty,\infty}$ with vertex set $\mathbb{Z}$ such that any two integer points $i<j$ are connected by an edge if and only if 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mated-CRT connection}
\begin{split}
&\left(\inf_{t\in[i-1,i]}L(t)\right)\vee\left(\inf_{t\in[j-1,j]}L(t)\right)\leq \inf_{t\in[i,j-1]}L(t)\quad\text{or}\\
&\left(\inf_{t\in[i-1,i]}R(t)\right)\vee\left(\inf_{t\in[j-1,j]}R(t)\right)\leq \inf_{t\in[i,j-1]}R(t).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Note that \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection} always holds if $j-i=1$. The integer points $i<j$, with $j-i>1$, are connected by two edges if both conditions in \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection} hold. We may embed $\mathcal{G}_{-\infty,\infty}$ so that an edge connecting $i<j$, with $j-i=1$, is a segment on $\mathbb{R}$, and any other edge given by the first (resp.\ second) condition in \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection} is embedded in $\mathbb{H}^{+}$ (resp.\ $\mathbb{H}^{-}$). (See \cite[Figure 1]{GMS19} for a further explanation of how this embedding is defined, in particular why the edges do not cross.)

For each infinite $\sqrt{2}$-mated-CRT map $\mathcal{G}_{-\infty,\infty}$, its associated orientation $\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-\infty,\infty}$ consists of all the directed edges in $\vv{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{G}_{-\infty,\infty})$ that go from the point $i$ to the point $j$ with $i<j$. The pair $(\mathcal{G}_{-\infty,\infty},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-\infty,\infty})$ is called the \textbf{directed infinite $\sqrt{2}$-mated-CRT map}.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:bound mated crt}
For each $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, let $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$ be the induced $\sqrt{2}$-mated-CRT map on the points $1,2,\dots,n$. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant upper and lower bound mated crt}
\mathbb{P}\Big[n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-5}\leq \max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{8}\Big] = 1-O_{n}(n^{-p}),\quad\text{for every }p>0. 
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- conjecture-1: body_md -->
\label[conjecture]{conj:minimal dimension}
Let $\Phi$ and $\eta$ be as above. Then almost surely, the minimal Hausdorff dimension with respect to the $\sqrt{2}$-LQG metric (determined by $\Phi$) of a directed path from $\eta(0)$ to $\eta(1)$ is $d_{\sqrt{2}}/3$, where $d_{\sqrt{2}}$ is the Hausdorff dimension of the $\sqrt{2}$-LQG metric.
<!-- end conjecture-1 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Mullin procedure}
Let $n$ be an arbitrary non-negative integer and let $\mathcal{Z}:\{0,1,\dots,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ be a two-dimensional walk with increments 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mullin procedure increments}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)\in\Big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\Big\},\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq n.    
\end{equation}
The \textbf{Mullin procedure} builds a sequence of triangulations $(\mathfrak{t}_{j})_{0\leq j\leq n}$ with no self loops through the following inductive procedure. For each $0\leq j\leq n$, the triangulation $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$ is of size $j$ and is called a \textbf{Mullin triangulation}. 

Take $\mathfrak{t}_{0}$ as a green edge with a red endpoint $u_{0}$ and a blue endpoint $v_{0}$, and take this edge as the active edge $\lambda_{0}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{0}$. (This is a triangulation of size 0.) For $1\leq j\leq n$, suppose we have defined the triangulation $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ that has a green active edge $\lambda_{j-1}$ with red endpoint $u_{j-1}$ and blue endpoint $v_{j-1}$ on its boundary $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$. We then inductively construct $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$ from $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ by adding a triangle incident to $\lambda_{j-1}$ in the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ as follows. 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{construction:add one red} Suppose we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk add one}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1) = (1,0).    
\end{equation}
Then add a red vertex $u_{j}$ in the interior of the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$, connect $u_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ with a red edge, and connect $u_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ with a green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $u_{j},u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $u_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ (so that $v_{j}=v_{j-1}$).

Similarly, suppose we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk add one blue}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1) = (0,1).    
\end{equation}
Then add a blue vertex $v_{j}$ in the interior of the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$, connect $v_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ with a blue edge, and connect $v_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ with a green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $v_{j},u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $v_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ (so that $u_{j}=u_{j-1}$).

\item\label{construction:minus one} Suppose we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk minus one red}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1) = (-1,0).    
\end{equation}
Consider the edge along $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ preceding $\lambda_{j-1}$, i.e.\ the edge along $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ that shares the same red vertex $u_{j-1}$ with $\lambda_{j-1}$. (When $j=1$ this edge is taken to be  $\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{j-1}$.) 
There are two cases.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item\label{minus one:non empty red} This edge is a red edge that has two red endpoints $x$ and $u_{j-1}$. Then connect $x$ to $v_{j-1}$ with a new green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $x,u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $x$ to $v_{j-1}$ (so that $u_{j}=x$ and $v_{j}=v_{j-1}$).
\item\label{minus one:empty red}
This edge is not a red edge. Then add a red vertex $u_{j}$ in the interior of the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$, connect $u_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ with a yellow edge, and connect $u_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ with a green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $u_{j},u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $u_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ (so that $v_{j}=v_{j-1}$).
\end{enumerate}

Similarly, suppose we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:walk minus one blue}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1) = (0,-1).    
\end{equation}
Consider the edge along $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ following $\lambda_{j-1}$, i.e.\ the edge along $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$ that shares the same blue vertex $v_{j-1}$ with $\lambda_{j-1}$. (When $j=1$ this edge is taken to be  $\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{j-1}$.) 
There are two cases.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item\label{minus one:non empty blue} This edge is a blue edge that has two blue endpoints $y$ and $v_{j-1}$. Then connect $y$ to $u_{j-1}$ with a green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $y,u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $y$ to $u_{j-1}$ (so that $u_{j}=u_{j-1}$ and $v_{j}=y$).
\item\label{minus one:empty blue}
This edge is not a blue edge. Then add a blue vertex $v_{j}$ in the interior of the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{j-1}$, connect $v_{j}$ to $v_{j-1}$ with a purple edge, and connect $v_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ with a green edge. Through this we have added a triangle with endpoints $v_{j},u_{j-1},v_{j-1}$ and obtain $\mathfrak{t}_{j}$. We take $\lambda_{j}$ as the added green edge connecting $v_{j}$ to $u_{j-1}$ (so that $u_{j}=u_{j-1}$).
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:Mullin bijection}
Let $n$ be an arbitrary non-negative integer and let $\mathcal{Z}:\{0,1,\dots,2n\}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ be a two-dimensional walk with increments
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mullin bijection increments}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)\in\Big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\Big\},\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq 2n.    
\end{equation}
We further require that $\mathcal{Z}$ starts and ends at $(0,0)$ and always stays in the closed first quadrant $[0,\infty)\times [0,\infty)$. The Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} builds a Mullin triangulation $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ of size $2n$ through an inductive procedure, and we further construct an ST map $(M,\vv{e},T)$ with $n$ edges from $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ in the following way. 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{Mul bij:black} Every blue edge of $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ is incident to two triangles, each of which contains exactly one red vertex. For every blue edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$, connect the associated two red vertices with a black edge which crosses the blue edge but no other edge of $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$; see the black edges in the bottom-left side of \Cref{fig:Mullin bijection}. 
\item\label{Mul bij:map} Let $M$ be the planar map whose vertex set consists of all the red vertices of $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ and whose edge set consists of all the red edges of $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ and all the black edges added in \ref{Mul bij:black}. 
\item The boundary $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$ contains only a red vertex $u$ and a blue vertex $v$. In addition, $v$ is contained in the interior of a face $f$ of $M$. Let $\vv{e}$ be the unique directed edge of $M$ such that $\vv{e}$ is incident to $f$, has $f$ on its left, and has $u$ as its terminal endpoint.
\item Let $T$ be formed by all the red edges in $\mathfrak{t}_{2n}$. 
\end{enumerate}
Then $(M,\vv{e},T)$ is the desired ST map with $n$ edges. Composing this construction with the Mullin procedure gives the Mullin bijection between walks $\mathcal{Z}$ on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ with $2n$ steps as above and ST maps with $n$ edges.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:four boundary of Mullin triangulation}
Let 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mullin procedure walk mullin triangulation}
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}):\{0,1,\dots,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}    
\end{equation}
be a walk with increments in $\big\{(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)\big\}$ and started at $(0,0)$, and let $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ be the associated Mullin triangulation of $\mathcal{Z}$ from the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure}. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length of four boundaries Mullin triangulation}
\begin{split}
\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n} = -\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j),\quad&\text{and}\quad \#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}=-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j),\\
\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n} = \mathcal{L}(n)-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j),\quad&\text{and}\quad \#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}=\mathcal{R}(n)-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Follows from the definition of the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} and an induction.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Mullin triangulation to Mullin map}
Let $\mathfrak{t}$ be an arbitrary finite Mullin triangulation.  For each blue or purple triangle, connect the unique red vertex of this triangle and the mid-point of the unique blue or purple edge of this triangle with a black half edge. If two black half edges share a common mid-point endpoint, then pair them into a single black edge.

Let $\overline{M}$ be the planar map with boundary whose vertex set consists of all the red vertices of $\mathfrak{t}$ and whose edge set consists of all the red and yellow edges of $\mathfrak{t}$ as well as all the added black edges and black half edges. All the yellow edges in $\mathfrak{t}$ are colored red in $\overline{M}$. After this, let $T$ consist of all the red edges of $\overline{M}$. Then $T$ is an ST on $\overline{M}$. In addition, $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}$ (which may be degenerate) forms a branch  of $T$ on $\partial\overline{M}$, and we keep track of it through the two corners $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$ of the external face of $\overline{M}$ that respectively contain the starting edge and active edge of $\mathfrak{t}$. To distinguish $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}$ from $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}$, we mark the vertex $\overline{v}$ on $\partial\overline{M}$ that corresponds to the unique common vertex of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}$ and $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}$; see the red squared vertex on the right-hand side of~\Cref{fig:Mullin triangulation to Mullin map}. Furthermore, $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}\cup\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}$ (which may be degenerate) corresponds to all the black half edges on $\partial\overline{M}$. To distinguish $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}$ from $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}$, we mark the half edge $\overline{e}$ that is contained in the last blue triangle incident to $\partial\mathfrak{t}$ when started from the active edge of $\mathfrak{t}$ and going around $\partial\mathfrak{t}$ in clockwise order, and we mark $\overline{e}$ as empty when there is no blue triangle in $\partial\mathfrak{t}$.

Every map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ that can be obtained by the operation in \Cref{def:Mullin triangulation to Mullin map} is called a \textbf{Mullin map}.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Mullin map} 
A \textbf{Mullin map} is a $6$-tuple $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ such that the following holds.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item $\overline{M}$ is a planar map with boundary and half edges. 
\item $T$ is an ST on $\overline{M}$.
\item $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$ are two (not necessarily distinct) corners of the external face of $\overline{M}$. In addition, suppose $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$, and suppose $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is the clockwise arc of $\partial\overline{M}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$. Then $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ does not contain any half edge, and all the edges in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ are in $T$.
\item The marked vertex $\overline{v}$ is a vertex of $\overline{M}$ lying on $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$.
\item The marked half edge $\overline{e}$ is empty (denoted by $\emptyset$) or a half edge of $\overline{M}$. (We always have that $\overline{e}$ is empty when $\overline{M}$ does not have any half edge.)
\end{enumerate}
The size of $\mathfrak{m}$, denoted by $s(\mathfrak{m})$, is equal to two times the number of edges of $\overline{M}$ plus the number of half edges of $\overline{M}$ minus the number of edges in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Mullin map to walk}
Let $n$ be an arbitrary positive integer and let $\mathfrak{m}_{n}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ be a Mullin map of size $n$. We associate it with a two-dimensional walk $\mathcal{Z}:\{0,1,\dots,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ started from $(0,0)$ and with increments   \begin{equation}\label{eq:Mullin map increments}
\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)\in\Big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\Big\},\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq n,    
\end{equation} 
in the following way. Let $\eta_{n}$ be the contour exploration of $\mathfrak{m}_{n}$. Then $\eta_{n}$ visits or crosses the edges or half edges of $\mathfrak{m}_{n}$ exactly $n$ times, and we enumerate the visits and crossings of $\eta_{n}$ by $X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}$. Then for all $1\leq j\leq n$, 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item If $X_{j}$ visits some edge in $T\setminus \partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ for the first time or visits some edge in $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, then $\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)=(1,0)$.
\item If $X_{j}$ visits some edge in $T\setminus \partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ for the second time or visits some edge in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, then $\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)=(-1,0)$.
\item If $X_{j}$ crosses some edge in $\overline{M}\setminus T$ for the first time or crosses some half edge in $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, then $\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)=(0,1)$. 
\item If $X_{j}$ crosses some edge in $\overline{M}\setminus T$ for the second time or crosses some half edge in $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, then $\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)=(0,-1)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label[proposition]{prop:Mullin bijection}
Fix $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Let $f_{1}$ be the mapping in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} from the set of walks on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ of $n$ steps that start at $(0,0)$ and have increments in $\big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\big\}$ to the set of Mullin triangulations of size $n$. Let $f_{2}$ be the mapping in \Cref{def:Mullin triangulation to Mullin map} from the set of Mullin triangulations of size $n$ to the set of Mullin maps of size $n$. Let $f_{3}$ be the mapping in \Cref{def:Mullin map to walk} from the set of Mullin maps of size $n$ to the set of walks on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ of $n$ steps that start at $(0,0)$ and have increments in $\big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\big\}$. Then $f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}$ are all bijections.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
It follows from the definitions that $f_{3}\circ f_{2}\circ f_{1}$, $f_{2}\circ f_{1}\circ f_{3}$ and $f_{1}\circ f_{3}\circ f_{2}$ are all identity mappings, and so $f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}$ are all bijections.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:four boundary of Mullin map}
Let 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mullin procedure walk mullin map}
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}):\{0,1,\dots,n\}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}    
\end{equation}
be a walk with increments in $\big\{(1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)\big\}$ and started at $(0,0)$, and let $\mathfrak{m}_{n}$ be the associated Mullin map from the slightly generalized Mullin bijection. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length of four boundaries Mullin map}
\begin{split}
\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{n} = -\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j),\quad&\text{and}\quad \#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}=-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j),\\
\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{n} = \mathcal{L}(n)-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j),\quad&\text{and}\quad \#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}=\mathcal{R}(n)-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Mullin map orientation}
For an arbitrary Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$, we associate it with an orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ in the following way. Recall that $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is the clockwise arc of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item Every edge in $T\setminus\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is doubly oriented. Let $\vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}$ be the set of these directed edges.  
\item Every edge in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is singly oriented clockwise along $\partial\mathfrak{m}$. Let $\vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\partial}$ be the set of these directed edges.
\item Every edge in $\overline{M}\setminus T$ is singly oriented from the first crossing point of $\eta$ to the second crossing point of $\eta$. Let $\vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathsf{b}}$ be the set of these directed edges.
\end{itemize}
We set 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mullin map orientation}
\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}} = \vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}\cup \vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\partial}\cup \vv{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathsf{b}}.   
\end{equation}
The pair $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ is called the \textbf{directed Mullin map}. (Half edges are not oriented in any direction, and any directed path cannot use a half edge.)
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:uniform marked point}
Fix $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, and let $\mathfrak{m}_{n}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ be a uniform Mullin map of size $n$. Recall that $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is the clockwise arc of $\partial\overline{M}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$. Then given $(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2})$, the conditional distribution of $\overline{v}$ is uniform over vertices in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$, and the conditional distribution of $\overline{e}$ is uniform over the union of $\emptyset$ (empty) and all the half edges of $\overline{M}$. Furthermore, the orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n}}$ associated with $\mathfrak{m}_{n}$ only depends on $(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2})$, and is independent of the choices of $\overline{v}$ and $\overline{e}$ given $(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2})$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Follows directly from \Cref{def:Mullin map,def:Mullin map orientation}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:no clockwise directed cycle}
For any finite Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ with corresponding orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}, there is no clockwise directed simple cycle $P$ in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $\eta$ be the contour exploration of $\mathfrak{m}$ defined before \Cref{def:Mullin map to walk}. By \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}, each edge $e$ of $\overline{M}\setminus T$ is singly oriented, and its associated directed edge $\vv{e}$ is oriented from the first crossing point between $\eta$ and $e$ to the second crossing point between $\eta$ and $e$. Because $\eta$ always keeps $T$ to its left, for every such directed edge $\vv{e}$, the first crossing of $\eta$ to $e$ is always from the right of $\vv{e}$ to the left of $\vv{e}$.

Now let $P$ be a directed simple cycle in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$. Because $T$ is an ST of $\overline{M}$, $P$ contains an edge in $\overline{M}\setminus T$. This means that $\eta$ crosses $P$ at least once. Suppose the first crossing of $\eta$ to $P$ happens at a directed edge $\vv{e}$ associated with an edge $e$ in $\overline{M}\setminus T$. Then we have just proved that this first crossing is from the right of $\vv{e}$ to the left of $\vv{e}$. Meanwhile, because $\eta$ starts at a corner of the external face of $\overline{M}$, this first crossing is also from the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminusP$ to the unique bounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminusP$. This means that the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminusP$ is to the right of $P$, and thus $P$ goes in counterclockwise order.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:no cycle determines orientation}
Let $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ be any finite Mullin map. Then the orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ given by \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation} is the unique orientation of $\mathfrak{m}$ such that (1) every edge in $T\setminus\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is doubly oriented; (2) every edge in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is singly oriented clockwise along $\partial\mathfrak{m}$; (3) every edge in $\overline{M}\setminus T$ is singly oriented, and (4) there is no clockwise directed simple cycle in $\mathfrak{m}$. Furthermore, the counterclockwise arc of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$, excluding all the half edges, is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We first prove the first claim. Clearly $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ is an orientation of $\mathfrak{m}$ that satisfies the requirements given by \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle}. Now consider any orientation $\vv{E}$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ that satisfies the requirements. Take an arbitrary edge $e$ of $\overline{M}\setminus T$, which is singly oriented in both $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}$ by requirement (3). There is a unique branch $B_{e}$ of $T$ that connects the two endpoints of $e$, and the union of $B_{e}$ and $e$ forms a simple loop $I_{e}$ in $\mathbb{C}$. By requirements (1) and (2), the edges in $B_{e}$ are oriented in the same way in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}$. Under this common orientation, each edge of $T\setminus\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ in $B_{e}$ is doubly oriented, and each edge of $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ in $B_{e}$ is oriented clockwise along $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ so that there is an infinite curve contained in the external face of $\mathfrak{m}$ that connects the left side of $e$ to $\infty$. Therefore, orienting $B_{e}$ by the clockwise order of $I_{e}$ gives a directed path in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}$. By requirement (4), $e$ must be oriented by the counterclockwise order of $I_{e}$ in both $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}$. So $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}$ agree on all the edges in $\mathfrak{m}$, and the first claim is proved. 

We then prove the second claim. Take an arbitrary edge $e$ that lies on the counterclockwise arc of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$. If $e$ also lies in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$, then $e$ is a cut edge of $\overline{M}$ and is oriented away from $x^{1}$ and towards $x^{2}$. If $e$ lies in $T\setminus\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$, then $e$ is doubly oriented. If $e$ lies in $\overline{M}\setminus T$, then define $I_{e}$ as in the above paragraph. We have proved that $e$ must be oriented by the counterclockwise order of $I_{e}$, and thus it is also oriented by the counterclockwise order of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ by considering an infinite curve contained in the external face of $\mathfrak{m}$ that connects $e$ to $\infty$. So in any case $e$ is at least singly oriented by the counterclockwise order of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$, and the second claim is proved.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:aj bj are increasing}
Let $(\mathfrak{m}_{n},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n}})$ be an arbitrary finite directed Mullin map of size $n$, and let $P$ be a directed simple path from a vertex in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ to a vertex in $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$. Suppose $P$ has length $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. For every $1\leq j\leq k$, let $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ be the $j$-th directed edge on $P$ where $(a_{j},b_{j})$ is its time pair. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:increasing sequence}
0\leq a_{1}< b_{1}\leq a_{2}< b_{2}\leq\cdots\leq a_{j}< b_{j}\leq\cdots \leq a_{k}< b_{k}\leq n.   \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The first and last inequalities are clear from definition. In addition, we know $a_{j}<b_{j}$ for every $j\in\{1,2,\dots,k\}$ by the definition of time pair. So we only need to prove $b_{j}\leq a_{j+1}$ for every $1\leq j\leq k-1$. We are going to prove it by induction.

\underline{\textbf{Basic setup}.} Let $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ be the Mullin triangulation of size $n$ associated with $\mathfrak{m}_{n}$ via the Mullin bijection. For every $0\leq i\leq n$, we use $\mathfrak{t}_{i}$ to denote the Mullin triangulation of size $i$ obtained by running $i$ steps of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$; use $(\mathfrak{m}_{i},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}})$ to denote the directed Mullin map of size $i$ associated with $\mathfrak{t}_{i}$; and use $u_{i}$ to denote the red endpoint of the active edge $\lambda_{i}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{i}$. Set $a_{k+1}=b_{k}$. Recall that $P$ is simple; that the $j$-th directed edge $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ on $P$ points from $u_{a_{j}}$ to $u_{b_{j}}$ for every $1\leq j\leq k$; and that $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$ for every $1\leq j\leq k$. In light of these, for all $j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that $1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}+1\leq k+1$, we use $P(u_{a_{j_{1}}},u_{b_{j_{2}}})$ to denote the part of $P$ from $u_{a_{j_{1}}}$ to $u_{b_{j_{2}}}$. When $j_{1}=j_{2}+1$, $P(u_{a_{j_{1}}},u_{b_{j_{2}}})$ is just a single point $u_{a_{j_{1}}}=u_{b_{j_{2}}}$. 

Fix $1\leq j\leq k-1$. Suppose we already know that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:increasing sequence induction hypothesis}
0\leq a_{1}< b_{1}\leq a_{2}< b_{2}\leq\cdots\leq a_{j}< b_{j}.
\end{equation}
We then prove $b_{j}\leq a_{j+1}$. We consider the three possible cases of $\mathcal{Z}(b_{j})-\mathcal{Z}(b_{j}-1)$ associated with $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$; see the top row of \Cref{fig:aj bj are increasing} for an illustration. If $\mathcal{Z}(b_{j})-\mathcal{Z}(b_{j}-1)=(1,0)$, then $u_{b_{j}}$ is not a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}-1}$, and is just added in the $b_{j}$-th step of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$. Since $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, we must have $b_{j}\leq a_{j+1}$ in this case. So we only need to consider the case $\mathcal{Z}(b_{j})-\mathcal{Z}(b_{j}-1)=(-1,0)$ and the case $\mathcal{Z}(b_{j})-\mathcal{Z}(b_{j}-1)=(0,-1)$. We give a robust argument that treats both these cases, which only slightly depends on the position of the starting point $u_{a_{1}}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ of $P$ and the position of the ending point $u_{b_{k}}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ of $P$. Consider the point set $I$ defined as follows. If $u_{a_{1}}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ is incident to an edge in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$, we let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:union pll}
I:=P(u_{a_{1}},u_{b_{j}})\cup\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}};
\end{equation}
while if $u_{a_{1}}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ is incident to a half edge $\overline{e}_{\text{start}}$ in $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, we let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:union plr}
I:=P(u_{a_{1}},u_{b_{j}})\cup\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{e}_{\text{start}}\cup\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}\cup\lambda_{0}
\end{equation}
where $\lambda_{0}$ is the green starting edge of $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$. Thanks to the induction hypothesis in \eqref{eq:increasing sequence induction hypothesis}, $P(u_{a_{1}},u_{b_{j}})$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}}}\big)$. So wherever $u_{a_{1}}$ is, $I$ is always a point set that is contained in the unique bounded closed region with boundary $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$.

\underline{\textbf{Suppose $a_{j+1}< b_{j+1}\leq b_{j}$.}} Then $u_{b_{j+1}}$ lies in $\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}}$ as $b_{j+1}\leq b_{j}$. Since $u_{b_{j+1}}$ is the ending point of $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ which starts at $u_{a_{j+1}}=u_{b_{j}}$, the vertex $u_{b_{j+1}}=u_{a_{j+2}}$ must lie in the closure of a bounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$; see \Cref{fig:aj bj are increasing} bottom middle. Now if the ending point $u_{b_{k}}$ of $P$ in $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ is incident to an edge in $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$, consider $P(u_{a_{j+2}},u_{b_{k}})$; while if $u_{b_{k}}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{n})$ is incident to an half edge $\overline{e}_{\text{end}}$ in $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}_{n}$, consider $P(u_{a_{j+2}},u_{b_{k}})$ followed by the half edge $\overline{e}_{\text{end}}$ pointing towards its non-vertex endpoint. In any case, we obtain a directed segment $\widetilde{P}$ that ends at a point on $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}\cup\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$, which is contained in the closure of the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$. Using that $I$ is contained in the unique bounded closed region with boundary $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$, $\widetilde{P}$ must end at a point in the closure of the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$. Since we already know that $\widetilde{P}$ starts at the point $u_{a_{j+2}}=u_{b_{j+1}}$ which lies in the closure of a bounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$, it follows from topological reasons that $I$ has to intersect with $\widetilde{P}$, and thus with $P(u_{a_{j+2}},u_{b_{k}})$ as well.

Consider the first hitting point of $P(u_{a_{j+2}},u_{b_{k}})$ to $I$. Whatever $I$ is as in \eqref{eq:union pll} or as in \eqref{eq:union plr}, since $(\lambda_{0}\setminus\{u_{0}\})\cup\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ cannot be hit or crossed by a red, yellow or black edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$, this hitting point must be $u_{b_{i}}$ for some $j+1\leq i\leq k$ that lies on $P(u_{a_{1}},u_{b_{j}})\cup\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$. In addition, because $P$ is simple, $u_{b_{i}}$ cannot lie on $P(u_{a_{1}},u_{b_{j}})$. So $u_{b_{i}}$ lies on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$. Consider the directed path $P_{C}$ formed by $P(u_{a_{j+1}},u_{b_{i}})$ followed by the clockwise boundary arc of $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$ from $u_{b_{i}}$ to $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$. Because we have assumed $a_{j+1}<b_{j+1}\leq b_{j}$, the directed edge $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{b_{j}}$. This implies that $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ cannot lie on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}\cup\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$ as edges on this boundary arc are only singly oriented towards $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, and also implies that $P(u_{a_{j+1}},u_{b_{i}})$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}},\vv{E}_{b_{j}})$ given the minimality of $i$. Since $P$ and 
$\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$ are both simple, combining the above two facts further implies that $P_{C}$ is a clockwise directed simple cycle in $(\mathfrak{m}_{b_{j}},\vv{E}_{b_{j}})$, a contradiction to \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle}. So we have excluded the possibility $a_{j+1}< b_{j+1}\leq b_{j}$.

\underline{\textbf{Suppose $a_{j+1}<b_{j}<b_{j+1}$.}} Then $a_{j+1}<b_{j+1}-1$, and $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ cannot be red. So $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ is a black directed edge. Let $\overline{e}_{1}$ (resp.\ $\overline{e}_{2}$) be the half edge in $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$ contained in the triangle added in the $a_{j+1}$-th (resp.\ $b_{j+1}$-th) step of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$. Since $a_{j+1}<b_{j}$, the triangle added in the $a_{j+1}$-th step of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ has to lie in $\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}
$, and thus further in the closure of a bounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$ as well; see \Cref{fig:aj bj are increasing}. On the other hand, since $b_{j+1}>b_{j}$, the triangle added in the $b_{j+1}$-th step of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ does not lie in $\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$. Using that $I$ is contained in the unique bounded closed region with boundary $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{b_{j}}$, this triangle needs to be contained in the closure of the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$.  Combining the above two facts yields that $\vv{e}_{(a_{j+1},b_{j+1})}$, formed by the union of $\overline{e}_{1}$ and $\overline{e}_{2}$ that respectively lies in one of the aforementioned triangles, has an interior intersection point with $I$. Whatever $I$ is as in \eqref{eq:union pll} or as in \eqref{eq:union plr}, this is a violation to the fact that in $\mathfrak{t}_{n}$, red, yellow, black, green edges and purple edges on $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ do not cross each other in the interior. So we have also excluded the possibility $a_{j+1}<b_{j}<b_{j+1}$, and the conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:directed geodesic}
For any directed map $(M,\vv{E})$ and any two (not necessarily distinct) vertices $x,x'$ on $M$, a \textbf{directed geodesic in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $x'$} is a directed path in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $x'$ that has the minimal length among all the directed paths from $x$ to $x'$. (The directed geodesic degenerates into a single point when $x=x'$.) When $M$ is a planar map with boundary and $x,x'$ both lie on $\partial M$, the union of all directed geodesics in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $x'$ forms, if it is not empty or a single vertex, a directed planar map with boundary. In this case, the \textbf{leftmost directed geodesic in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $x'$} is the clockwise boundary arc of the aforementioned directed planar map from $x$ to $x'$.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:geodesic map}
Suppose $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ is a finite Mullin map, and there is a directed geodesic $P$ in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$. We want to define the \textbf{left geodesic map} $\mathsf{M}^{L}$ and the \textbf{right geodesic map} $\mathsf{M}^{R}$, as well as the \textbf{directed left geodesic map} $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and the \textbf{directed right geodesic map} $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$ in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ cut by $P$. Here $\mathsf{M}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},P)$ (resp.\ $\mathsf{M}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},P)$) will be a planar map $\overline{M}^{L}$ (resp.\ $\overline{M}^{R}$) with boundary and half edges decorated with some marked edges that form the set $T^{L}$ (resp.\ $T^{R}$) and with a marked directed boundary path $P$. In addition, $\vv{E}^{L}$ (resp.\ $\vv{E}^{R}$) will be an orientation on $\mathsf{M}^{L}$ (resp.\ $\mathsf{M}^{R}$) where half edges are not oriented in any direction so that any directed path cannot use a half edge. We forget about the corners $C^{1},C^{2}$, the marked vertex $\overline{v}$ and the marked half edge $\overline{e}$ in $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$.

Let $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ be the clockwise arc of $\partial\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, and let $I=\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})\cupP\subseteq\mathbb{C}$. The left set with respect to $P$ is defined to be the the union of $P$ with the closure of the union of all the bounded connected components of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$, and the right set with respect to $P$ is defined to be the union of $P$ with the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I$. We then make the following definition.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item The vertex set of $\overline{M}^{L}$ is the intersection of the vertex set of $\overline{M}$ with the left set, and similarly for the set of edges and half edges of $\overline{M}^{L}$. (We emphasize that vertices and edges on $P$ also lie in $\overline{M}^{L}$.) In addition, $T^{L}$ and $\vv{E}^{L}$ are respectively the restriction of $T$ and $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{L}$.
\item The vertex set of $\overline{M}^{R}$ is the intersection of the vertex set of $\overline{M}$ with the right set, and similarly for the set of edges and half edges of $\overline{M}^{R}$. (We emphasize that vertices and edges on $P$ also lie in $\overline{M}^{R}$.) In addition, $T^{R}$ is the union of $P$ and the restriction of $T$ to $\overline{M}^{R}$, and $\vv{E}^{R}$ is the restriction of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{R}$, except that every edge in $P$ is only singly oriented according to the orientation of $P$ no matter whether it is doubly oriented in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ or not.
\end{itemize}
(In particular, when $x^{1}=x^{2}$ and $P$ degenerates into a single vertex, the left set degenerates into a single point and the right set is $\mathbb{C}$. In this case $\overline{M}^{L}$ and $T^{L}$ both degenerate into a single vertex and $\vv{E}^{L}=\varnothing$, while $\overline{M}^{R}=\overline{M}$, $T^{R}=T$ and $\vv{E}^{R}=\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$.)
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:ST in left and right}
Let $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ be an arbitrary Mullin map associated with the orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$. Suppose $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$. Let $P$ be an arbitrary directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, which exists since the clockwise boundary arc $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$. (We allow the case $x^{1}=x^{2}$ and $P$ degenerates into a single vertex.) Let $\mathsf{M}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},P)$ and $\mathsf{M}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},P)$ be the left and right geodesic maps in $\mathfrak{m}$ cut by the directed geodesic $P$. Then $T^{L}$ is an ST in $\overline{M}^{L}$, and $T^{R}$ is an ST in $\overline{M}^{R}$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Since $T$ is an ST of $\mathfrak{m}$ and $T^{R}$ contains all the edges of $P$, it follows that if $T^{L}$ is an ST of $\overline{M}^{L}$, then $T^{R}$ is an ST of $\overline{M}^{R}$. Hence, it suffices to prove that $T^{L}$ is an ST of $\overline{M}^{L}$. By definition, $T^{L}$ is the intersection of $T$ with the left set. It has the same vertex set as $\overline{M}^{L}$, forms no cycle, and contains no half edge. So it remains to show that $T^{L}$ is connected. Assuming the opposite, there is a vertex $u$ of $\mathsf{M}^{L}$ such that the unique branch of $T$ in $\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{2}$ to $u$ uses an edge of $T$ in $\overline{M}^{R}\setminusP$. So there must exist a segment $S$ of this branch with different endpoints $x$ and $x'$ on $P$ that is contained in $\overline{M}^{R}\setminusP$ and only intersects $P$ at $x$ and $x'$. Now since $P$ goes from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, all the edges of $T$ in $\overline{M}^{R}\setminusP$ do not lie on the clockwise boundary arc $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$. As a result, all the edges in $S$ are doubly oriented by \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}. Therefore, if $x$ is visited before $x'$ in $P$, then the part of $P$ from $x$ to $x'$ followed by the segment $S$ oriented from $x'$ to $x$ forms a clockwise directed simple cycle in $\mathfrak{m}$; while if $x'$ is visited before $x$ in $P$, then the part of $P$ from $x'$ to $x$ followed by the segment $S$ oriented from $x$ to $x'$ forms a clockwise directed simple cycle in $\mathfrak{m}$. In either case this contradicts \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle}, and the conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:Boltzmann Mullin map}
For $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$, we define the \textbf{Boltzmann distribution with parameter $(\ell,r)$} on Mullin maps. The random map $\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r)$ following this distribution is called the \textbf{Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$}. The distribution is defined such that, for some normalizing constant $C_{\ell,r}\in(0,\infty)$ and for each Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$ of size $n$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Boltzmann Mullin map}
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r)=\mathfrak{m}]=\frac{4^{-n}}{C_{\ell,r}}\mathbbm{1}\{\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}=\ell,\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}=r,\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}=\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}=0\}.    
\end{equation}
A Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$ induces an orientation $\vv{E}(\ell,r)$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}, and the pair $\big(\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r),\vv{E}(\ell,r)\big)$ is called a \textbf{directed Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$}.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:Boltzmann from walk}
Let $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$. Let \begin{equation}\label{eq:one-sided infinite walk from 0}
\mathcal{Z}=(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}):\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
\end{equation} 
be a one-sided infinite simple random walk started at $(0,0)$. Let $\tau$ be the smallest $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of tau Boltzmann}
\mathcal{L}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n-1}\mathcal{L}(j)-1\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j),
\end{equation}
if such a time exist; otherwise set $\tau=\infty$. (We will prove later in \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time} that $\tau$ is almost surely finite, but currently we do not need this.) On the event $\{\tau<\infty\}$,
let $\mathfrak{m}$ be the Mullin map obtained by applying the Mullin bijection to $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,\tau-1]}$. Then conditioned on the event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditioning event Boltzmann}
E_{\ell,r}:=\big\{\tau<\infty,\ \mathcal{L}(\tau-1)=-\ell,\ \mathcal{R}(\tau)=-r\big\},    
\end{equation}
the conditional law of $\mathfrak{m}$ given $E_{\ell,r}$ is exactly the law of a Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$. In particular, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:choice of cB}
C_{\ell,r}=4\mathbb{P}[E_{\ell,r}].    
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Clearly $E_{\ell,r}$ has positive probability for all $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$. It follows by definition that on $E_{\ell,r}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of eB}
\mathcal{L}(\tau-1)=\min_{0\leq j\leq \tau-1}\mathcal{L}(j)=-\ell,\quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{R}(\tau-1)=\min_{0\leq j\leq \tau-1}\mathcal{R}(j)=-r.    
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:def of tau Boltzmann}, $\mathcal{Z}(\tau)-\mathcal{Z}(\tau-1)=(-1,0)$. We then know from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length of four boundaries of m}
\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}=\ell,\quad\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}=r,\quad\text{and}\quad\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}=\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}=0.    
\end{equation}
Furthermore, that $\mathfrak{m}$ is of size $n$ is equivalent to $\tau=n+1$, and in this case every possible sample of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}$ has probability $4^{-n}$ before conditioning on $E_{\ell,r}$. The conclusion then follows from the Mullin bijection in \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection} and comparing to \Cref{def:Boltzmann Mullin map}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:conditional independence of Boltzmann Mullin map}
Let $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$, and let $\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r)=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},x^{2},\emptyset)$ be a Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$ associated with the orientation $\vv{E}(\ell,r)$, where $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$. Since the clockwise boundary arc $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ of $\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r)$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ is a directed path in $\big(\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r),\vv{E}(\ell,r)\big)$, the leftmost directed geodesic (\Cref{def:directed geodesic}) from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ in $\big(\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r),\vv{E}(\ell,r)\big)$ exists, and we denote it by $P$. (We allow the case $x^{1}=x^{2}$ and $P$ degenerates into a single point.) Let $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$ be the directed left and right geodesic map (\Cref{def:geodesic map}) in $\big(\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r),\vv{E}(\ell,r)\big)$ cut by $P$. Then $\mathsf{M}^{L}$ determines $\vv{E}^{L}$ and $\mathsf{M}^{R}$ determines $\vv{E}^{R}$, and $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$ are conditionally independent given the length $|P|$ of $P$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let us first consider the case $|P|=0$. This means that $x^{1}=x^{2}$ and $P$ degenerates into a single point. By \Cref{def:geodesic map}, in this case the geodesic maps $\mathsf{M}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},P)$ and $\mathsf{M}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},P)$ satisfy that $\overline{M}^{L}$ and $T^{L}$ both degenerate into a single point and $\vv{E}^{L}=\varnothing$, while $\overline{M}^{R}=\overline{M}$, $T^{R}=T$ and $\vv{E}^{R}=\vv{E}(\ell,r)$. So the result clearly holds. We then consider the case $|P|>0.$ Fix positive integers $\ell\geq s$ and a non-negative integer $r$. 


\underline{\textbf{We first give some basic setup.}}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item Let $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$ be the set of all Mullin maps $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},x^{2},\emptyset)$ where $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1}$ and $C^{2}$ such that (1) $\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}=\ell$; (2) $\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}=r$; and (3) $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}}(x^{1},x^{2})=s$. 
\item Let $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}$ be the set of all Mullin maps $\mathfrak{m}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},C^{1,L},C^{2,L},x^{2},\emptyset)$ --- with corresponding orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation} --- where $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1,L}$ and $C^{2,L}$ such that (1) $\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}^{L}=\ell$; (2) $\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}^{L}=0$ (so that $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ does not have half edges); (3) $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}(x^{1},x^{2})=s$; and (4) the counterclockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ is the unique directed geodesic from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ in $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$.
\item Let $\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$ be the set of all Mullin maps $\mathfrak{m}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},C^{1,R},C^{2,R},x^{2},\emptyset)$ --- with corresponding orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation} --- where $x^{1}$ and $x^{2}$ are respectively the apex of $C^{1,R}$ and $C^{2,R}$ such that (1) $\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}^{R}=s$; (2) $\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}^{R}=r$;  (3) $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}(x^{1},x^{2})=s$; and (4) the clockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ is a directed geodesic from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ in $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$. 
\end{itemize}

\underline{\textbf{We now describe a bijection $\Phi$ from $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$ to $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}\times\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$.}} See \Cref{fig:conditional independence lemma} for an illustration. Given a Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$, with corresponding orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}, we take the leftmost directed geodesic $P$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ that has length $s$. We then apply \Cref{def:geodesic map} and consider the directed left and right geodesic maps $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$ cut by $P$, where $\mathsf{M}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},P)$ and $\mathsf{M}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},P)$.  Let $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ be the clockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, which is also contained in $\mathsf{M}^{L}$. Since $s\geq 1$, $x^{1}\neq x^{2}$, and $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ and $P$ both contain at least one edge. Take $C^{1,L}$ (resp.\ $C^{2,L}$) as the unique corner of the external face of $\overline{M}^{L}$ that is incident to $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ and has $x^{1}$ (resp.\ $x^{2}$) as its apex. Take $C^{1,R}$ (resp.\ $C^{2,R}$) as the unique corner of the external face of $\overline{M}^{R}$ that is incident to $P$ and has $x^{1}$ (resp.\ $x^{2}$) as its apex. Then let $\mathfrak{m}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},C^{1,L},C^{2,L},x^{2},\emptyset)$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},C^{1,R},C^{2,R},x^{2},\emptyset)$. By \Cref{lem:ST in left and right}, $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ are Mullin maps. We further show that their orientations $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$ and $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$ given by \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation} are exactly $\vv{E}^{L}$ and $\vv{E}^{R}$. 
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{(Left).} For $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$, note that $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is the clockwise boundary arc from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$ in both $\mathfrak{m}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$, and recall that $\vv{E}^{L}$ is exactly the restriction of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{L}$. So in $\vv{E}^{L}$: (1) every edge in $T^{L}\setminus \partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is doubly oriented; (2) every edge in $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ is singly oriented clockwise along $\partial\overline{M}^{L}$; (3) every edge in $\mathfrak{m}^{L}\setminus T$ is singly oriented; and we know from \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle} that (4) there is no clockwise directed simple cycle. It then follows from \Cref{lem:no cycle determines orientation} that $\vv{E}^{L}$ is the same as $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$.
\item \textbf{(Right).} For $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$, recall that $\vv{E}^{R}$ is exactly the restriction of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{R}$ except that all the edges in $P$ are singly oriented in $\overline{M}^{R}$ according to the orientation of $P$. Because $P$ is a directed path from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, all the edges in $T^{R}\setminusP$ do not lie on $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$, and are thus doubly oriented in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}^{R}$. In addition, the orientation of $P$ is aligned with the clockwise order of $\partial\overline{M}^{R}$. Furthermore, every edge in $\mathfrak{m}^{R}\setminus T^{R}$ is singly oriented in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ and $\vv{E}^{R}$. Finally, since $P$ is a directed path in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$, $\vv{E}^{R}$ is a subset of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$, and we know from \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle} that there is no clockwise directed simple cycle in $(\mathfrak{m}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$. It then follows from \Cref{lem:no cycle determines orientation} that $\vv{E}^{R}$ is the same as $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$.
\end{itemize}
By comparing the definitions, we then know that $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ respectively belong to $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}$ and $\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$.

\smallskip

\underline{\textbf{We then describe the inverse bijection $\Phi^{-1}$ from $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}\times\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$ to $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$.}} See \Cref{fig:conditional independence lemma} for an illustration. Suppose $\mathfrak{m}^{L}=(\overline{M}^{L},T^{L},C^{1,L},C^{2,L},x^{2},\emptyset)$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}=(\overline{M}^{R},T^{R},C^{1,R},C^{2,R},x^{2},\emptyset)$ respectively belong to $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}$ and $\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$. Because the counterclockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ from the apex of $C^{1,L}$ to the apex of $C^{2,L}$ and the clockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ from the apex of $C^{1,R}$ to the apex of $C^{2,R}$ both have length $s$, and $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ does not have any half edges, we can glue $\overline{M}^{L}$ and $\overline{M}^{R}$ along the two aforementioned boundary arcs to obtain the planar map $\overline{M}$ with boundary and half edges. The two identified arcs become a path $P$ from $x^{1}$ (the identified apex of $C^{1,L}$ and $C^{1,R}$) to $x^{2}$ (the identified apex of $C^{2,L}$ and $C^{2,R}$). Take $T$ as the union of $T^{L}$ and $T^{R}\setminusP$. Then because $T^{L}$ and $T^{R}$ are respectively an ST of $\overline{M}^{L}$ and $\overline{M}^{R}$, $T$ is an ST of $\overline{M}$. Let $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ be the clockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$, which is also a clockwise boundary arc of $\overline{M}$. Since $s\geq 1$, $x^{1}\neq x^{2}$, and $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ contains at least one edge. Take $C^{1}$ (resp.\ $C^{2}$) as the unique corner of the external face of $\overline{M}$ that is incident to $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ and has $x^{1}$ (resp.\ $x^{2}$) as its apex. Then $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},x^{2},\emptyset)$ is a Mullin map. Suppose $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$ and $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$ are the associated orientations of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ given by \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}. We then show that the orientation $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ given by \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation} is exactly $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}\cup\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$. 
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item Take $\vv{E}^{L}$ as the restriction of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{L}$. By the verbatim argument as in \textbf{(Left)} above, we have $\vv{E}^{L}=\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$.
\item Note that $P$ is the counterclockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1,L}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2,L}$. By \Cref{lem:no cycle determines orientation}, $P$ is a directed path in $\vv{E}^{L}$ and thus in $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ by the first item.
\item Take $\vv{E}^{R}$ as the restriction of $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}$ to $\overline{M}^{R}$ except that all the edges in $P$ are singly oriented in $\overline{M}^{R}$ according to the orientation of $P$ from $x^{1}$ to $x^{2}$. By the verbatim argument as in \textbf{(Right)} above and the second item, we have $\vv{E}^{R}=\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$.
\end{itemize}
Combining the above three items gives that $\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\vv{E}^{L}\cup\vv{E}^{R}$. By comparing the definitions, we then know that $\mathfrak{m}$ belongs to $\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$. Finally, it is clear that the mappings $\Phi$ and $\Phi^{-1}$ are inverses of each other.

\smallskip

\underline{\textbf{Conclusion.}} Recall now the definition of the size of a Mullin map in \Cref{def:Mullin map}. Suppose $\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{\ell,r,s}$, and $(\mathfrak{m}^{L},\mathfrak{m}^{R})=\Phi(\mathfrak{m})\in\mathcal{M}_{\ell,s}^{L}\times\mathcal{M}_{s,r}^{R}$. By comparing to definitions, the size $s(\mathfrak{m})$ of $\mathfrak{m}$ is equal to the size $s(\mathfrak{m}^{L})$ of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ plus the size $s(\mathfrak{m}^{R})$ of $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$ minus $s$, where the minus $s$ comes from the fact that the leftmost directed geodesic $P$ in $\mathfrak{m}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$ has $s$ edges. These $s$ edges occur in both $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$, while all the other edges or half edges in $\mathfrak{m}$ only occur in one of $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$. As a result, it follows from \Cref{def:Boltzmann Mullin map} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:product factorization}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathfrak{m}(\ell,r)=\mathfrak{m}\mid |P|=s\Big]= \frac{4^{-s(\mathfrak{m})}}{C_{\ell,r}\mathbb{P}[|P|=s]} = \frac{4^{s}}{C_{\ell,r}\mathbb{P}[|P|=s]}\cdot 4^{-s(\mathfrak{m}^{L})}\cdot 4^{-s(\mathfrak{m}^{R})}.   
\end{equation}
Now let $(\mathsf{M}^{L},\vv{E}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}^{R},\vv{E}^{R})$ be the directed left and right geodesic maps cut in $\mathfrak{m}$ by $P$. In the construction of $\Phi$, we see that $\mathsf{M}^{L}$ and $\mathsf{M}^{R}$ respectively determine $\mathfrak{m}^{L}$ and $\mathfrak{m}^{R}$, which further determine $\vv{E}^{L}=\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{L}}$ and $\vv{E}^{R}=\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}^{R}}$. The conclusion then follows from \eqref{eq:product factorization}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}
The following holds almost surely. For every $k\geq 0$, all the times $n$ that $x_{-k}$ is the red endpoint of $\lambda_{n}$ are when 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation left}
\mathcal{L}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j)=-k,    
\end{equation}
and all the times $n$ that $y_{k}$ is the blue endpoint of $\lambda_{n}$ are when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation right}
\mathcal{R}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j)=-k.    
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix $k\geq 0$. Because $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ is the increasing union of $(\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n})_{n\geq 0}$, for every $n\geq 0$, $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ is contained in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ while all the edges in $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ are not contained in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. So if $x_{k}$ is the red endpoint of $\lambda_{n}$, then $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ contains exactly $k$ edges and $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ degenerates into a single point. Similarly, if $y_{k}$ is the blue endpoint of $\lambda_{n}$, then $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ contains exactly $k$ edges and $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n}$ degenerates into a single point. The conclusion then follows from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin triangulation}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:disjoint orientation}
Almost surely, for every $a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $\vv{E}_{0,a}$ and $\vv{E}_{a,\infty}$ are disjoint.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:boundary cut vertex}
For each $n\geq 0$, the $n$-th active edge $\lambda_{n}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ is called a \textbf{critical edge} of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ if $u_{n}\in\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ and $v_{n}\in\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. When $\lambda_{n}$ is a critical edge, we say $u_{n}$ is a \textbf{boundary cut vertex} of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$.
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:boundary cut vertex}
The following holds almost surely. For all $n\geq 0$, if $\lambda_{n}$ is a critical edge of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, then for every vertex $u$ in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ and every vertex $u'$ in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ that is not a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$, each path in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ that starts at $u$ and ends at $u'$ must go through $u_{n}$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Recall that the red, blue, and green edges in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ do not cross each other, and each black edge in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ crosses exactly one blue edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ but no red or green edges. In addition, for all $n\geq 0$, the edge set of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ consists of all the red edges in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$, all the black edges in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ that cross a blue edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$, and some half edges. 

Now the case $n=0$ is trivial. Suppose $\lambda_{n}$ is a critical edge of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ where $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. By definition of the critical edge, $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ consists of four parts: the green starting edge $\lambda_{0}$ which is part of $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$; $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ which is contained in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$; the green active edge $\lambda_{n}$; and $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ which is part of $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. See \Cref{fig:boundary cut vertex} for an illustration. Furthermore, by our assumption, $u$ lies in the closure of the unique bounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$, and $u'$ lies in the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$. So every path in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ that starts at $u$ and ends at $u'$ has to exit $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ and enter the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ at some point. In order not to cross $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ or the interior of a green edge, every such path can only exit $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ at $u_{n}$. The conclusion thus follows.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-16: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:cone time}
We call $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ a \textbf{simultaneous running minimum time of $\mathcal{Z}$} if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cone time}
\mathcal{L}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j).
\end{equation}
Denote by $S$ the set of simultaneous running minimum times, and write $\#S$ for the number of elements in $S$. Set $T_{0}$ as $0$. For each $1\leq k\leq \#S$, let $T_{k}$ be the $k$-th smallest number in $S$, and for each $k>\#S$, let $T_{k}$ be $\infty$. For each $1\leq k\leq \#S$, the time $T_{k}$ is called the \textbf{$k$-th simultaneous running minimum time of $\mathcal{Z}$}.
<!-- end definition-16 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:basic property of cone time}
 For all $k\geq 0$, the time $T_{k}$ is a stopping time for $\mathcal{Z}$. In addition, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, conditioning on the event $\{T_{k}<\infty\}$, the time increment $T_{k+1}-T_{k}$ has the same distribution as $T_{1}$, and the random excursions 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:i.i.d. conditional law}
\Big(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+T_{j-1})-\mathcal{Z}(T_{j-1})\Big)|_{[0,T_{j}-T_{j-1}]},\quad j=1,2,\dots,k    
\end{equation}
are conditionally i.i.d.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label[proposition]{prop:infinitely many cone time}
Almost surely, there are infinitely many simultaneous running minimum times of $\mathcal{Z}$. That is, almost surely $\#S=\infty$ and all the $T_{k},k\geq 0,$ are finite. In particular, almost surely, there are infinitely many boundary cut vertices in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We first prove the first claim. Recall that $\mathcal{Z}$ starts at the origin. It then follows from \Cref{def:cone time} and the reversibility and symmetry of $\mathcal{Z}$ that for each $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prob that n in S}
\mathbb{P}[n\in S] = \mathbb{P}\Big[\mathcal{L}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j),\mathcal{R}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j)\Big] = \mathbb{P}\Big[\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j)=0\Big].
\end{equation}
By \cite[Theorem 1 and Example 2]{DW15}, there exists a positive constant $c$ such that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, the last term of \eqref{eq:prob that n in S} is at least $c/n$\footnote{There is $1/16$ probability that $\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,0)$ and $\mathcal{Z}(2)-\mathcal{Z}(1)=(0,1)$. Assuming this, we can then apply \cite[Theorem 1 and Example 2]{DW15} on the random walk $\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(2),j\geq 2$, the cone $K=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}$, and the point $x=(1,1)$. In particular, because $x\in K_{+}$, $V(x)$ is positive by the statement of \cite[Theorem 1]{DW15}.}.
As a result, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:expectation is infinite}
\mathbb{E}[\#S]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}[n\in S]\geq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c}{n}=\infty.     \end{equation}
On the other hand, it follows from \Cref{lem:basic property of cone time} that if $\mathbb{P}[T_{1}=\infty]$ is positive, then $\#S$ is a geometric random variable with success probability $\mathbb{P}[T_{1}=\infty]$, which has finite expectation. So $T_{1}$ should be almost surely finite. The first claim then follows from \Cref{lem:basic property of cone time}. Given this, the second claim then follows from \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:cone time and LQG}
The continuum counterpart of a simultaneous running minimum time for $\mathcal{Z}$ is a time at which the two coordinates of a 2d Brownian motion attain a simultaneous running minimum. Such a time is a $\pi/2$-cone time for the time reversal of the 2d Brownian motion, in the sense of \cite{Shi85}. It is proved in \cite[Theorem 1]{Shi85} that such $\pi/2$-cone times almost surely do not exist for the 2d Brownian motion. Heuristically speaking, the simultaneous running minimum times for $\mathcal{Z}$ are very sparse, so they do not give rise to simultaneous running minimum times of the limiting 2d Brownian motion. This is similar in spirit to the fact that 2d random walk is recurrent but 2d Brownian motion almost surely never returns to its starting point.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Recall the definition of the directed FUI Mullin map $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ given in~\Cref{subsec:local limit}. Note that deleting all the half edges in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ gives exactly $M_{0,\infty}$, and $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ and $M_{0,\infty}$ are associated with the same orientation $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$. As a result, we only need to prove the slight variation of \Cref{thm:Busemann exists} where we replace $M_{0,\infty}$ by $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$.

We then prove that the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}$ as in the statement of \Cref{thm:Busemann exists} (except that we have $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ in place of $M_{0,\infty}$) exists and is unique. Given the last paragraph, the uniqueness is clear as we have fixed $\mathcal{X}(0)=0$, and we only need to prove existence. For $m\geq 0$, define the times $T_{m}$ as in \Cref{def:cone time}, which we know are almost surely all finite by \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time}.
