We further know from \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex,def:cone time} and \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} that $u_{T_{m}},m\geq 0$ are boundary cut vertices.

Because $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ is the union of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n},n\geq 0,$ and $T_{m}\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, there exists $m_{1}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that $x_{0}$ and $x_{k}$ are both vertices of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,T_{m_{1}(k)}}$ and thus of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{1}(k)}}$. By \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, the two infinite branches in $T_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{0}$ and $x_{k}$ to $\infty$, which are both infinite directed paths in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$, both need to go through $u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}}$. This implies that there exist finite directed paths in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{0}$ and $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}}$. As a result, we can define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:choice of Busemann function}
\mathcal{X}(k)=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}}),\quad\text{for every }k\in\mathbb{Z}.  \end{equation}

It remains to prove that the function $\mathcal{X}$ defined above satisfies the relation in \eqref{eq:Busemann exists} with $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ in place of $M_{0,\infty}$. Fix $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$ and a sequence of distinct vertices $\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}$ as in the statement. (The existence of such a sequence has already been proved.) Take $m_{2}(k,k')=m_{1}(k)\vee m_{1}(k')$ so that $x_{k}$ and $x_{k'}$ are both vertices of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{2}(k,k')}}$. Since $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{2}(k,k')}}$ is finite, there exists $J\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that for every $j\geq J$, $w_{j}$ is not a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{2}(k,k')}}$. Then again by \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, for every $j\geq J$, all the directed paths in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{0}$ and $x_{k}$ (resp.\ $x_{0}$ and $x_{k'}$) to $w_{j}$ have to go through $u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}}$ (resp.\ $u_{T_{m_{1}(k')}}$). So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann sum cut vertex k}
\begin{split}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},w_{j})&=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}})+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}},w_{j})\\
&=\mathcal{X}(k)+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}})+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{T_{m_{1}(k)}},w_{j})\\
&=\mathcal{X}(k)+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w_{j}),
\end{split}
\end{equation}
and similarly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann sum cut vertex k'}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k'},w_{j})=\mathcal{X}(k')+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},w_{j}).
\end{equation}
Subtracting the two equations above yields the desired conclusion.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- definition-17: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:infinite directed geodesic}
For any infinite directed map $(M,\vv{E})$ and any vertex $x$ on $M$, an \textbf{infinite directed geodesic in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $\infty$} is an infinite directed path in $(M,\vv{E})$ started from $x$ such that each of its finite segments is a directed geodesic in $(M,\vv{E})$ from the initial vertex of the segment to the terminal vertex of the segment.  When $M$ is an infinite planar map with boundary, the union of all infinite directed geodesics in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $\infty$, if it is not empty, forms a directed planar map with boundary. In this case, the \textbf{leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(M,\vv{E})$ from $x$ to $\infty$} is the unique directed infinite boundary arc of the aforementioned directed planar map that is started from $x$ and has the aforementioned directed planar map on its right.
<!-- end definition-17 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label[corollary]{cor:infinite directed geodesic}
The following holds almost surely. For each $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, there exists an infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. In particular, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, there is a unique leftmost infinite directed geodesic $P_{k}$ in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Define the times $T_{m},m\geq 0,$ as in \Cref{def:cone time}, which we know are almost surely all finite by \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time}.  We further know from \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex,def:cone time} and \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} that $u_{T_{m}},m\geq 0,$ are boundary cut vertices. Now fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. Because $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ is the union of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n},n\geq 0$, and $T_{m}\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$, there exists $m_{1}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that $x_{k}$ is a vertex of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,T_{m_{1}(k)}}$ and thus of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{1}(k)}}$. By \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, the infinite branch in $T_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$, which is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$, needs to go through $u_{T_{m}}$ for all $m\geq m_{1}(k)$. This implies that for every $m\geq m_{1}(k)$, there exists a finite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m}}$. 

For every $m\geq m_{1}(k)$, let $P_{k,m}$ be the leftmost directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m}}$ as defined in \Cref{def:directed geodesic}. Then \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} implies that for all $m'\geq m\geq m_{1}(k)$, $P_{k,m'}$ needs to go through $u_{T_{m}}$. We then know from the uniqueness of leftmost directed geodesics that for all $m'\geq m\geq m_{1}(k)$, the part of $P_{k,m'}$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m}}$ is exactly $P_{k,m}$. Thus we can define an infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$ by declaring that for every $m\geq m_{1}(k)$, this infinite directed geodesic coincides with $P_{k,m}$ until the first time when it hits $u_{T_{m}}$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label[corollary]{cor:two parts of leftmost infinite directed geodesic}
The following holds almost surely. Suppose $k\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ satisfy that $x_{k}$ is a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}}$. Then the leftmost infinite directed geodesic $P_{k}$ (\Cref{cor:infinite directed geodesic}) visits $u_{T_{m}}$ exactly once. Furthermore, write $P_{k}(x_{k},u_{T_{m}})$ for the part of $P_{k}$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m}}$, and write $P_{k}(u_{T_{m}},\infty)$ for the part of $P_{k}$ from $u_{T_{m}}$ to $\infty$. Then $P_{k}(x_{k},u_{T_{m}})$ is the leftmost directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}},\vv{E}_{0,T_{m}})$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{T_{m}}$, and $P_{k}(u_{T_{m}},\infty)$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{m},\infty},\vv{E}_{T_{m},\infty})$ from $u_{T_{m}}$ to $\infty$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
That $P_{k}$ visits $u_{T_{m}}$ exactly once follows from \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} and the fact that $P_{k}$ is an infinite directed geodesic. Again by \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, $P_{k}$ cannot visit a vertex of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ that is not a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}}$ before visiting $u_{T_{m}}$, so $P_{k}(x_{k},u_{T_{m}})$ is completely contained in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}},\vv{E}_{0,T_{m}})$. In addition, $P_{k}(u_{T_{m}},\infty)$ cannot visit a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}}$ apart from at its starting point $u_{T_{m}}$, or by \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} it will visit $u_{T_{m}}$ again after doing so, which contradicts that $P_{k}$ visits $u_{T_{m}}$ exactly once. So $P_{k}(u_{T_{m}},\infty)$ is also completely contained in $(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{m},\infty},\vv{E}_{T_{m},\infty})$. The second claim then follows from the uniqueness of leftmost infinite directed geodesics.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label[corollary]{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic}
The following holds almost surely. For all $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$, the infinite directed geodesics $P_{k}$ and $P_{k'}$ will coalesce, that is, they will meet at some point and agree thereafter. Furthermore, for all $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k')$ is equal to the difference of lengths of the parts of $P_{k}$ and $P_{k'}$ before they coalesce.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
See \Cref{fig:coalescence of directed geodesic} for an illustration. Fix $k,k'\in\mathbb{Z}$. Let $m_{1}(k)$ and $m_{1}(k')$ be the same as in the proof of \Cref{cor:infinite directed geodesic}. Take $m_{2}(k,k')=m_{1}(k)\vee m_{1}(k')$ so that $x_{k}$ and $x_{k'}$ are both vertices of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m_{2}(k,k')}}$. Then it follows from \Cref{cor:two parts of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} that $P_{k}$ and $P_{k'}$ both visit $u_{T_{m_{2}(k,k')}}$ exactly once, and $P_{k}(u_{T_{m_{2}(k,k')}},\infty)$ is the same as $P_{k'}(u_{T_{m_{2}(k,k')}},\infty)$. Now in the definition of the Busemann function in \Cref{thm:Busemann exists}, we can take $\{w_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}$ as the sequence of vertices that lie in $P_{k}(u_{T_{m_{2}(k,k')}},\infty)=P_{k'}(u_{T_{m_{2}(k,k')}},\infty)$, and the conclusion then follows from this definition.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:every geodesic into two parts}
The following holds almost surely for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. Let $n$ be an arbitrary non-negative integer such that $x_{k}$ is a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$. Let $T=T(k,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ be the first time when the $T$-th directed edge in $P_{k}$ does not belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$. (Such a time always exists because $P_{k}$ goes to $\infty$ and $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ is bounded.) Then the part of $P_{k}$ after its $T$-th directed edge is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty},\vv{E}_{n,\infty})$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$. Recall below \Cref{lem:no cycle determines orientation} the definitions of the vertex sets $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m})$ and $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m})$ associated with a finite Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}$. Then $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,j}),j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ increase to $\{x_{k}\}
_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$. So $x_{k}$ lies in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ if and only if $x_{k}$ belongs to $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$. 

We then want to apply \Cref{lem:aj bj are increasing}. Recall above \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} that for each $j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $u_{j}$ is the unique red endpoint of the $j$-th active edge $\lambda_{j}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. Also recall from the end of \Cref{subsec:local limit} the definition of the time pair of a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. For every $j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, let $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ be the $j$-th directed edge on $P_{k}$ where $(a_{j},b_{j})$ is its time pair. In particular, $u_{a_{1}}=x_{k}$. 

Consider the times $T_{m},m\geq 0,$ as in \Cref{def:cone time}, which we know are almost surely all finite by \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time}. Since $T_{m}\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$, there exists $m_{1}(k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that for all $m\geq m_{1}(k)$, $x_{k}$ is a vertex in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}})$ but is not equal to $u_{T_{m}}$. Fix one arbitrary such $m$. Then it follows from \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} that $P_{k}$ has to go through $u_{T_{m}}$. Thus, as we have assumed $x_{k}\neq u_{T_{m}}$, $u_{T_{m}}$ is equal to $u_{b_{j(m)}}$ for some $j(m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Then by applying \Cref{lem:aj bj are increasing} to the part of $P_{k}$ from $u_{a_{1}}=x_{k}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}})$ to $u_{b_{j(m)}}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m}})$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aj bj are increasing until m}
0\leq a_{1}<b_{1}\leq a_{2}<b_{2}\leq\cdots \leq a_{j(m)}<b_{j(m)}.    
\end{equation}
Since $j(m)\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$, we further obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aj bj are increasing until infinity}
0\leq a_{1}<b_{1}\leq a_{2}<b_{2}\leq\cdots \leq a_{j}<b_{j}\leq \cdots.    
\end{equation}
Now take the smallest $T\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that $b_{T}>n$. As $a_{j},b_{j},1\leq j\leq T-1$ are all no more than $n$, the first $T-1$ directed edges $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})},1\leq j\leq T-1$ on $P_{k}$ all belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$. As $b_{T}>n$, the $T$-th directed edge $\vv{e}_{(a_{T},b_{T})}$ on $P_{k}$ does not belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$. As $a_{j},b_{j},j\geq T+1$ are all at least $n$, for every $j\geq T+1$, the $j$-th directed edge $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ on $P_{k}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{n,\infty}$. The conclusion thus follows.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$. In $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, the blue vertex $y_{k}$ is connected with a green edge to the red vertex $x_{k}$ and to the red vertex $x_{k+1}$. Consider the green edges incident to $y_{k}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ going in clockwise order, starting from the green edge connecting $y_{k}$ to $x_{k}$ and ending at the green edge connecting $y_{k}$ to $x_{k+1}$. Suppose these green edges are $g_{0},g_{1},\dots,g_{d}$, $d\geq 0$. For each $0\leq j\leq d$, write $u_{k,j}$ as the red endpoint of $g_{j}$ (so that $u_{k,0}$ is $x_{k}$ and $u_{k,d}$ is $x_{k+1}$).

Fix an arbitrary $1\leq j\leq d$. Consider the unique triangle $A$ that contains $g_{j-1}$ and lies immediately to the left of $g_{j-1}$ when we stand at $u_{k,j-1}$ and look toward $y_{k}$. If $A$ is red, then let $e_{j}$ be the unique red edge of $A$, which lies in both $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ and $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$. Because $g_{j}$ is the edge immediately after $g_{j-1}$ in clockwise order among all the green edges incident to $y_{k}$, the two red endpoints of $e_{j}$ are exactly $u_{k,j-1}$ and $u_{k,j}$. If $A$ is blue, then the blue edge of $A$ cannot lie on $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ since otherwise $g_{j}$ cannot exist. So there is another blue triangle $A'$ that shares the same blue edge with $A$. Again, since $g_{j}$ is the edge immediately after $g_{j-1}$ in clockwise order among all the green edges incident to $y_{k}$, the unique red endpoint of $A$ is $u_{k,j-1}$, and the unique red endpoint of $A'$ is $u_{k,j}$. In addition, there must be a black edge $e_{j}$ in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ connecting $u_{k,j-1}$ with $u_{k,j}$ that crosses the shared blue edge of $A$ and $A'$. See the left-hand side of~\Cref{fig:exponential tail} for an illustration.

For every $1\leq j\leq d$, let $\vv{e_{j}}$ be the directed edge associated with $e_{j}$ that is oriented from $u_{k,j-1}$ to $u_{k,j}$. We claim that for each $1\leq j\leq d$, $\vv{e_{j}}$ is contained in the orientation set $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$. We consider again the two cases in the above paragraph. In the first case, $e_{j}$ is red. Suppose $e_{j}$ lies in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. Then since $g_{j}$ is the edge immediately after $g_{j-1}$ in clockwise order among all the green edges incident to $y_{k}$, $u_{k,j-1}$ is closer to $x_{0}$ than $u_{k,j}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, so $\vv{e_{j}}$ is contained in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$. Otherwise, $e_{j}$ does not lie in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ and is doubly oriented, so $\vv{e_{j}}$ is contained in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ as well. In the second case, $e_{j}$ is black, and crosses a blue edge that is incident to $y_{k}$. Since $y_{k}$ lies on $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, there is an infinite path from $y_{k}$ to $\infty$ that is contained in the external face of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. This infinite path, following part of the blue edge that $\vv{e_{j}}$ crosses, gives an infinite path from the right side of $\vv{e_{j}}$ to $\infty$ which is contained in the external face of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$. See the dashed curve in \Cref{fig:exponential tail} right for an illustration. Since $e_{j}$ does not lie in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, as it is black, the existence of this infinite path implies that the right boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$, after excluding all the half edges and viewed as an infinite directed path started from $x_{0}$, contains the directed edge $\vv{e_{j}}$. In addition, we know that this right boundary arc is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ by applying the last result of \Cref{lem:no cycle determines orientation} to $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ and then sending $n$ to $\infty$. So $\vv{e_{j}}$ is contained in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$, and the claim holds.

Therefore, the path $\vv{e_{1}}\vv{e_{2}}\cdots\vv{e_{d}}$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $x_{k+1}$. See \Cref{fig:exponential tail} right for an illustration. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp difference no more than d}
\mathcal{X}(k)\leq \mathcal{X}(k+1)+d\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{X}(k+1)-\mathcal{X}(k)\geq -d.  
\end{equation}
So to prove \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:exponential negative tail for positive} it suffices to prove that the distribution of $d$ has an exponential tail.

Recall the definition of the $n$-th active edge $\lambda_{n}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ above \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}. Note that $(\lambda_{n})_{n\geq 0}$ enumerate all the green edges in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. By \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}, we know that $d+1$ is equal to the number of times $n$ when 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:number of d}
\mathcal{R}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}(j)=-k.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal{R}$ is a lazy random walk, each time when \eqref{eq:number of d} holds, there is a 1/4 chance that the next step is a downward step for $\mathcal{R}$, in which case this condition never holds again. So $d$ is dominated by a geometric random variable (with values in $\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}$) with success probability 1/4. The conclusion then follows from the standard large deviation property of the geometric random variable.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
For each $k\leq 0$, the value of $\mathcal{X}(k)$ is at most $1$ plus the value of $\mathcal{X}(k-1)$ because the left boundary arc $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$, viewed as an infinite directed path started from $x_{0}$, is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- definition-18: body_md -->
\label[definition]{def:infinite geodesic map}
Consider the FUI Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}=(\overline{M}_{0,\infty},T_{0,\infty},C_{0,\infty})$ with corresponding orientation $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$. For every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, let $P_{k}$ be the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. For every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, we want to define the \textbf{left geodesic map} $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ and the \textbf{right geodesic map} $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$, as well as the \textbf{directed left geodesic map} $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ and the \textbf{directed right geodesic map} $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$ in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ cut by the infinite directed geodesic $P_{k}$. When $k=0$, we simply write $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L})$ for $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{0,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{0,L})$, and write $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})$ for $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{0,R})$.

Fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}$. Here $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}=(\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},T_{0,\infty}^{k,L},P_{k})$ (resp.\ $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}=(\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},T_{0,\infty}^{k,R},P_{k})$) will be an infinite planar map $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ (resp.\ $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$) with boundary and half edges decorated with some marked edges that form the set $T_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ (resp.\ $T_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$) and with a marked infinite directed boundary path $P_{k}$. In addition, $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ (resp.\ $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$) will be an orientation on $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ (resp.\ $\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$) where half edges are not oriented in any direction so that any directed path cannot use a half edge. (Note that we forget about the corner $C_{0,\infty}$ both in $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$.)

We first consider the case when $k\leq 0$. Let $I_{k}$ be the union of $P_{k}$ with the part of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. The left set with respect to $P_{k}$ is taken as the union of $P_{k}$ with the closure of the union of all the bounded connected components of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I_{k}$, and the right set with respect to $P_{k}$ is taken as the union of $P_{k}$ with the unique unbounded connected component of $\mathbb{C}\setminus I_{k}$. See \Cref{fig:infinite geodesic map} left for an illustration. We then make the following definition.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item The vertex set of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ is the intersection of the vertex set of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}$ with the left set, and similarly for the set of edges and half edges of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$. (We emphasize that vertices and edges on $P_{k}$ also lie in $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$.) In addition, $T_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ and $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$ are respectively the restriction of $T_{0,\infty}$ and $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ to $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}$.
\item The vertex set of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$ is the intersection of the vertex set of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}$ with the right set, and similarly for the set of edges and half edges of $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$. (We emphasize that vertices and edges on $P_{k}$ also lie in $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$.) In addition, $T_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$ is the union of $P_{k}$ and the restriction of $T_{0,\infty}$ to $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$, and $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$ is the restriction of $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ to $\overline{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}$, except that every edge in $P_{k}$ is only singly oriented according to the orientation of $P_{k}$ no matter whether it is doubly oriented in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ or not.
\end{itemize}
We then consider the case when $k>0$. In this case we let $I_{k}$ be the union of the following five parts: $P_{k}$; the green edge $\lambda_{\eta_{k}}$ with red endpoint $x_{k}$ and blue endpoint $y_{k}$; the part of $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ from $y_{k}$ to $y_{0}$; the green starting edge $\lambda_{0}$; and $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. See \Cref{fig:infinite geodesic map} right for an illustration. All the above definitions then remain verbatim.
<!-- end definition-18 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap}
Almost surely, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $P_{-k}$ is also the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$ from $x_{-k}$ to $\infty$, and $P_{k}$ is also the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. \underline{\textbf{We first prove that $P_{-k}$ is an infinite directed path in}} \underline{$(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$.} By applying \Cref{lem:every geodesic into two parts} to the vertex $x_{-k}$ that lies in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\tau_{k}}$, it suffices to prove that the first directed edge on $P_{-k}$ cannot belong to $\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$.

For the following argument, see \Cref{fig:directed geodesic in submap} left for an illustration. We know from \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} that $\tau_{k}$ is the first time when $x_{-k}$ is the red endpoint of the active edge. By the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure}, this means that $x_{-k}$ is not a red vertex in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\tau_{k}-1}$ and is just added in the $\tau_{k}$-th step of the Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. So the only edge in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\tau_{k}}$ that $x_{-k}$ is incident to is the edge on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ with endpoints $x_{-k}$ and $x_{-k+1}$. However, this edge is only singly oriented in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{-k+1}$ to $x_{-k}$. So the first directed edge on $P_{-k}$ cannot belong to $\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$, and thus $P_{-k}$ must be an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$.
 
\underline{\textbf{We then prove that $P_{k}$ is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$.}} By applying \\
\Cref{lem:every geodesic into two parts} to the vertex $x_{k}$ that lies in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}}$, it suffices to prove that the first directed edge on $P_{k}$ cannot belong to $\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}}$. Assume the opposite. 

For the following argument, see \Cref{fig:directed geodesic in submap} right for an illustration. Since $P_{k}$ goes to infinity and $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ is bounded, $P_{k}$ has to exit $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ at some point. Recall that the red, blue and green edges in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ do not cross each other, and each black edge in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ crosses exactly one blue edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ but no red or green edges. Now the boundary of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ consists of five parts: the green starting edge $\lambda_{0}$ which is part of $\partial\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$; $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ which is contained in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$; $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ containing only red edges; the green active edge $\lambda_{\eta_{k}}$; and $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ which is contained in $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. As $P_{k}$ cannot exit $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ from $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}\cup\{\lambda_{0}\}\cup\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, it can only exit $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ from $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$. Given that the first directed edge on $P_{k}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}}$, we obtain that there is some point on $P_{k}$ after $x_{k}$ that also lies on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$.

Let $z\in\mathcal{V}(\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}})$ be the first hitting point of $P_{k}$ after $x_{k}$ to $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$, and consider the directed path $P_{C}$ formed by the part of $P_{k}$ from $x_{k}$ to $z$ followed by the part of $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ from $z$ to $x_{k}$. Since the first directed edge on $P_{k}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}}$, this edge cannot lie on $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ as edges on this boundary arc of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ are only singly oriented towards $x_{k}$ in $\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}}$. In addition, it follows from the choice of $z$ that the part of $P_{k}$ from $x_{k}$ to $z$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}})$. Because $P_{k}$ and $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\eta_{k}}$ are both simple, combining the above two facts then implies that $P_{C}$ is a clockwise directed simple cycle in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}})$, a contradiction to \Cref{lem:no clockwise directed cycle}. Thus $P_{k}$ must be an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$.

\underline{\textbf{Conclusion.}} As we have proved that $P_{-k}$ must be an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$ and that $P_{k}$ must be an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$, the conclusion then follows from the uniqueness of leftmost infinite directed geodesics.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:left and right geodesic map characterization}
For every $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:left and right geodesic map stationary}
\big((\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}),(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R})\big)\quad\text{and}\quad \big((\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L}),(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R})\big)
\end{equation}
have the same law as $\big((\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L}),(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})\big)$. In addition, almost surely, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:left and right geodesic map characterization}
\begin{split}
&(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{-k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{-k,L})=(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L}),\quad (\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{-k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{-k,R})=(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}\cup\mathfrak{m}_{0,\tau_{k}},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}\cup\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}\cup\vv{E}^{\rm cross}_{\tau_{k}}),\\
&(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})=(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L}\cup\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L}\cup\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}}),\quad(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})=(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Fix $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. The first claim follows from the strong Markov property of $\mathcal{Z}$ and \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap}. The second claim follows from \Cref{def:infinite geodesic map} and \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap}, and the only subtlety that needs further explanation is the following. For $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{-k,R}=\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}\cup\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$, by \Cref{def:infinite geodesic map}, we require all the edges on $P_{-k}$ to be only singly oriented in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{-k,R}$ and in $\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}$ according to the orientation of $P_{-k}$, but we do not make any such requirement for directed edges in $\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$. However, it follows from \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap} that $P_{-k}$ only uses edges in $\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}$, and we further know from \Cref{lem:disjoint orientation} that $\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}$ and $\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$ are disjoint. So all the edges on $P_{-k}$ are still oriented in the same way in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{-k,R}$ and $\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R}\cup\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}}$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Because leftmost directed geodesics are unique, the leftmost infinite directed geodesics $P_{k},k\in\mathbb{Z}$ cannot cross each other. As a result, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, $P_{j}$, $j\leq k$ (resp.\ $P_{j},j\geq k$) are infinite directed paths in $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$). In addition, it follows from \Cref{lem:left and right geodesic map characterization} that when $k\leq 0$ (resp.\ $k>0$), $P_{k}$ and $P_{k-1}$ are determined by $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k-1,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k-1,R})$) in a manner that does not depend on $k$. Since \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} further implies that $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)$ is determined by $P_{k}$ and $P_{k-1}$ in a manner that does not depend on $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, when $k\leq 0$ (resp.\ $k>0$), $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)$ is determined by $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k-1,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k-1,R})$) in a manner that does not depend on $k$. Finally, \Cref{lem:left and right geodesic map characterization} implies that $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L}),k\leq 0$ have the same law, and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R}),k\geq 0$ have the same law. The conclusion thus follows.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label[proposition]{prop:independence of directed left and right geodesic map}
The directed geodesic maps $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})$ are independent.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Because leftmost directed geodesics are unique, the leftmost infinite directed geodesics $P_{k},k\in\mathbb{Z}$ cannot cross each other. As a result, for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, $P_{j}$, $j\leq k$ (resp.\ $P_{j},j\geq k$) are infinite directed paths in $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$). Since \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} further implies that $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1)$ is determined by $P_{k}$ and $P_{k-1}$ for each $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, we obtain that for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, the increments $\mathcal{X}(j)-\mathcal{X}(j-1),j\leq k$ are measurable with respect to $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$, and the increments $\mathcal{X}(j)-\mathcal{X}(j-1), j\geq k+1$ are measurable with respect to $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$. 

Now for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$, it follows from the strong Markov property of $\mathcal{Z}$ that (1) $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\tau_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}})$ is independent with $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$; (2) $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}})$ is independent with $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$; and (3) $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty})$ and $(\mathfrak{m}_{\eta_{k},\infty},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty})$ both have the same law as $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. Combining the third fact (3) with \Cref{prop:independence of directed left and right geodesic map} implies that for each $k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$,  $(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L})$ is independent with $(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R})$, and  $(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L})$ is independent with $(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R})$. Given this, the first two facts (1) and (2) then further imply that for each $k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$, $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\tau_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\tau_{k}})$, $(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{L})$, $(\mathsf{M}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\tau_{k},\infty}^{R})$ are jointly independent, and $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\eta_{k}},\vv{E}_{0,\eta_{k}})$, $(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{L})$, $(\mathsf{M}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{\eta_{k},\infty}^{R})$ are jointly independent. Consequently, it follows from \Cref{lem:left and right geodesic map characterization} that for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, the directed geodesic maps $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{k,R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{k,R})$ are independent. The conclusion then follows from the last paragraph.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:existence of Boltzmann Mullin map}
For every $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, conditioned on $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}$, the conditional law of $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{k_{m}}},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}})$ is the law of a directed Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $\big(-\mathcal{L}(T_{k_{m}}),-\mathcal{R}(T_{k_{m}})\big)$ defined in \Cref{def:Boltzmann Mullin map}.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{Basic setup.}} Fix $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. A finite nearest-neighbour trajectory is a finite sequence $w=\big(w(0),w(1),\dots,w(n)\big)$ of points on $\mathbb{Z}^2$, for some $n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite nearest-neighbour trajectory}
w(j)-w(j-1)\in
\big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\big\},
\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq n.
\end{equation}
For every such $w$, we write $\operatorname{len}(w):=n$, and write $w^{L}$ and $w^{R}$ respectively as the left and right coordinate of $w$ so that $w(j)=\big(w^{L}(j),w^{R}(j)\big)$ for all $0\leq j\leq \operatorname{len}(w)$. For $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ with $\ell<m$, let
$\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$ be the set of all finite nearest-neighbour trajectories
$w$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Alr condition 1}
w(0)=(0,0),\quad
w\big(\operatorname{len}(w)\big)=(-\ell,-r),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Alr condition 2}
w^L\big(\operatorname{len}(w)\big)
 =\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)}w^L(j),
\quad
w^R\big(\operatorname{len}(w)\big)
 =\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)}w^R(j).
\end{equation}
These are precisely the possible realizations of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}$ on the
event $\mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r).$

A one-sided infinite nearest-neighbour trajectory $q$ is a one-sided infinite sequence $q=\big(q(j)\big)_{j\geq 0}$ of points on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:infinite nearest-neighbour trajectory}
q(j)-q(j-1)\in
\big\{(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\big\},
\quad\text{for all }j\geq 1.
\end{equation}
For every such $q$, we write $q^{L}$ and $q^{R}$ respectively as the left and right coordinate of $q$ so that $q(j)=\big(q^{L}(j),q^{R}(j)\big)$ for all $j\geq 1$. For a one-sided infinite nearest-neighbor trajectory
$q=(q(j))_{j\geq 0}$ started from $(0,0)$ and every $\ell<m$, let (we assume $\inf\varnothing=\infty$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigma lm}
\sigma_{\ell,m}(q)
 :=\inf\big\{j\geq 1:q^L(j)=-(m-\ell)\big\}.
\end{equation}
For every $\ell<m$, let $\mathcal H_{\ell}$ be the event that $q$ has no simultaneous running
minimum time strictly between $0$ and $\sigma_{\ell,m}(q)$. Explicitly,
$q\in\mathcal H_{\ell}$ if there is no
$1\leq j\leq \sigma_{\ell,m}(q)-1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cone time for q}
q^L(j)=\min_{0\leq i\leq j}q^L(i)
\quad\text{and}\quad
q^R(j)=\min_{0\leq i\leq j}q^R(i).
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{Conditional independence of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}$ and $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}$.}} From now on, we fix arbitrary $\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ with $\ell<m$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of Zm}
\mathcal{Z}_{m}(j):=\mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}}+j)-\mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}}),
\quad\text{for all }j\geq 0.
\end{equation}
Fix $w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$ and write
$n=\operatorname{len}(w)$. For every measurable set $E$ of one-sided infinite nearest-neighbour trajectories, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prefix-future-factorization}
\mathbb{P}\Big[
 \mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}=w,\,
 \mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r),\,
 \mathcal{Z}_{m}\in E
 \Big]
 =4^{-n}\,
 \mathbb{P}_{(0,0)}
 \big[
 \mathcal{Z}\in E\cap\mathcal H_{\ell}
 \big].
\end{equation}
Indeed, since $w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$, its terminal time is a
simultaneous running minimum time. After concatenating $w$ with a future
trajectory $q$, this terminal time is the last simultaneous running
minimum time strictly before the left coordinate first reaches $-m$ if
and only if $q\in\mathcal H_{\ell}$. The independence of the increments
after the deterministic time $n$ therefore gives
\eqref{eq:prefix-future-factorization}. Notice that we apply independence
at the deterministic time $n$, not at the (non-stopping) time $T_{k_{m}}$.

Consequently, for every
$\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$ and every measurable set $E$ of one-sided
infinite nearest-neighbour trajectories,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prefix-future-factorization-summed}
\mathbb{P}\Big[
 \mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}\in\mathcal A,\,
 \mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r),\,
 \mathcal{Z}_{m}\in E
 \Big] 
 =\left(\sum_{w\in\mathcal A}
          4^{-\operatorname{len}(w)}\right)
   \mathbb{P}_{(0,0)}
   \big[
     \mathcal{Z}\in E\cap\mathcal H_{\ell}
   \big].
\end{equation}
Taking $\mathcal A=\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$ in
\eqref{eq:prefix-future-factorization-summed}, we obtain, for every
measurable set $E$ of one-sided infinite nearest-neighbour trajectories,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A=Alr}
\mathbb P\Big[
 \mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r),\,
 \mathcal{Z}_{m}\in E
 \Big] 
 =
\left(
\sum_{\widetilde w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}}
4^{-\operatorname{len}(\widetilde w)}
\right)
\mathbb P_{(0,0)}
\big[
\mathcal{Z}\in E\cap\mathcal H_{\ell}
\big].
\end{equation}
Combining this identity with
\eqref{eq:prefix-future-factorization} gives, for every
$w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$,
\[
\mathbb P\Big[
 \mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}=w,\,
 \mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r),\,
 \mathcal{Z}_{m}\in E
 \Big] =
\frac{4^{-\operatorname{len}(w)}}
{\displaystyle
 \sum_{\widetilde w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}}
 4^{-\operatorname{len}(\widetilde w)}}
\,
\mathbb P\Big[
 \mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r),\,
 \mathcal{Z}_{m}\in E
 \Big].
\]
Since this holds for every measurable set $E$ of one-sided infinite nearest-neighbour trajectories, the defining property of
conditional probability implies that, almost surely on the event
$\big\{\mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})=(-\ell,-r)\big\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional probability}
\mathbb{P}\left[
 \mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}=w
 \,\middle|\,
 \mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}
\right]
=
\frac{4^{-\operatorname{len}(w)}}
{\displaystyle
 \sum_{\widetilde w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}}
 4^{-\operatorname{len}(\widetilde w)}},
\quad\text{for all }
w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}.
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{We now translate the preceding description through the Mullin bijection.}} Fix $w\in\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$. Since the terminal point of $w$ is
$(-\ell,-r)$ and is a simultaneous running minimum, we have
\[
-\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)} w^L(j)=\ell,
\qquad
-\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)} w^R(j)=r,
\]
and
\[
w^L\big(\operatorname{len}(w)\big)
-\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)}w^L(j)=0,
\qquad
w^R\big(\operatorname{len}(w)\big)
-\min_{0\leq j\leq\operatorname{len}(w)}w^R(j)=0.
\]
It therefore follows from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} that
the Mullin map encoded by $w$ has lower-left and lower-right boundary
lengths $\ell$ and $r$, respectively, and has both upper boundary lengths
equal to zero.

Conversely, by the Mullin bijection in \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection} and
\Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map}, every Mullin map with these four
boundary lengths is encoded by a unique trajectory in
$\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$. Thus, the Mullin bijection restricts to a bijection
between $\mathcal A_{\ell,r}$ and the set of Mullin maps appearing in
the definition (\Cref{def:Boltzmann Mullin map}) of the Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$.

Moreover, the size of the Mullin map encoded by $w$ is
$\operatorname{len}(w)$. Hence, the conditional law obtained above
assigns to each such Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}$ a probability proportional
to $4^{-s(\mathfrak{m})}$.
By \Cref{def:Boltzmann Mullin map}, this is precisely the law of a
Boltzmann Mullin map with parameter $(\ell,r)$. Since the orientation
associated with a Mullin map is determined by the map through
\Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}, the same statement holds for the
corresponding directed Mullin map. Finally, on the event under
consideration, we have that $(\ell,r)=\big(-\mathcal{L}(T_{k_{m}}),-\mathcal{R}(T_{k_{m}})\big)$, which proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:conditional independence in Boltzmann beads}
For every $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R})$ are conditionally independent given $\mathcal{F}_{m}$.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Since $P_{0}$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $u_{0}=x_{0}$ to $\infty$, we know from \Cref{cor:two parts of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} that $P_{0}$ visits $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$ exactly once, and the part of $P_{0}$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m}}
}$, which we denote by $P_{0}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m}}})$, is exactly the leftmost directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{k_{m}}},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}})$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$. This part only depends on $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,T_{k_{m}}]}$ via the Mullin bijection, and has length  $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m}}})$. In addition, by comparing \Cref{def:geodesic map} with \Cref{def:infinite geodesic map}, we know that $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R})$ are the directed left and right geodesic maps of $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{k_{m}}},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}})$ cut by $P_{0}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m}}})$. 
The conclusion then follows from the conditional independence in~\Cref{lem:conditional independence of Boltzmann Mullin map} and~\Cref{lem:existence of Boltzmann Mullin map}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:monotonicity and tail triviality}
The $\sigma$-algebras $\mathcal{F}_{m}$, $m\geq 1,$ in \eqref{eq:def of sigma algebra} are decreasing in $m$. In addition, their intersection $\cap_{m\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{m}$ is trivial, i.e.~any event in this intersection $\sigma$-algebra has probability $0$ or $1$.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{We first prove that $\mathcal{F}_{m}$ is decreasing in $m$.}} We only need to prove that $\mathcal{F}_{m+1}$ is contained in $\mathcal{F}_{m}$ for every $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Fix $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Rewrite $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}$ and $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m+1}},\infty)}$ as follows
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rewrite again}
\begin{split}
&\big(\mathcal{L}_{m}(j),\mathcal{R}_{m}(j)\big)=\mathcal{Z}_{m}(j):=\mathcal{Z}(j+T_{k_{m}}),\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{for all }j\geq 0,\\ 
&\big(\mathcal{L}_{m+1}(j),\mathcal{R}_{m+1}(j)\big)=\mathcal{Z}_{m+1}(j):=\mathcal{Z}(j+T_{k_{m+1}}),\qquad\,\,\text{for all }j\geq 0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Also define 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:taum and tkm for nw}
\begin{split}
\tau(\mathcal{Z}_{m})&:=\min\big\{n\geq 0:\mathcal{L}_{m}(n)=-m-1\big\},\quad\text{and}\\
T(\mathcal{Z}_{m})&:=\max\big\{0\leq n<\tau(\mathcal{Z}_{m}):\mathcal{L}_{m}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}_{m}(j),\mathcal{R}_{m}(n)=\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{R}_{m}(j)\big\}.    
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Then by \Cref{def:cone time}, \eqref{eq:def of tauk and etak} and \eqref{eq:def of km}, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:km tells km+1}
T_{k_{m+1}}=T_{k_{m}}+T(\mathcal{Z}_{m})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{Z}_{m+1}(j)=\mathcal{Z}_{m}\big(j+T(\mathcal{Z}_{m})\big),\quad\text{for all }j\geq 0.
\end{equation}
So $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m+1}},\infty)}$ is measurable with respect to $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}$. Furthermore, \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} implies that each directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m+1}}}$ has to pass $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$. So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp summable}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m+1}}}) = \mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m}}})+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{T_{k_{m}}},u_{T_{k_{m+1}}}).
\end{equation}
In addition, each directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m+1}}}$ cannot visit a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,{T_{k_{m}}}}$ apart from its starting point $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$, or by \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, it will visit $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$ again after doing so, which contradicts the assumption of being a directed geodesic. As a result, each directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $u_{T_{k_{m}}}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m+1}}}$ is completely contained in $(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{k_{m}},\infty},\vv{E}_{T_{k_{m}},\infty})$, which is determined by $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m}},\infty)}$ from the Mullin bijection. This means that both terms on the right hand side of \eqref{eq:sdp summable} are measurable with respect to $\mathcal{F}_{m}$. So $\mathcal{F}_{m+1}$ is contained in $\mathcal{F}_{m}$.

\underline{\textbf{We then prove the tail triviality of $\mathcal{F}_{m}$, $m\geq 1$.}} By \Cref{lem:basic property of cone time}, the random variables
\begin{equation}\label{eq:i.i.d. variables}
X_{j}:=\Big(\mathcal{Z}(\cdot+T_{j-1})-\mathcal{Z}(T_{j-1})\Big)|_{[0,T_{j}-T_{j-1}]},\quad j\geq 1,  
\end{equation}
are i.i.d. We are going to use the Hewitt-Savage $0$-$1$ law on these i.i.d.~random variables. We know that all the $\mathcal{F}_{m},m\geq 1,$ are measurable with respect to the $\sigma$-algebra generated by $X_{j}$, $j\geq 1$, which is exactly the $\sigma$-algebra generated by $\mathcal{Z}$. Therefore, it suffices to prove that every event in $\cap_{m\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{m}$ remains unchanged after any finite permutation on $X_{j}$, $j\geq 1$. Fix one such permutation and assume that it is a permutation on the first $k\geq 1$ variables. We only need to prove that every event in $\cap_{m\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{m}$ remains unchanged after applying that permutation.

For a $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{F}$ on the space $\Omega$ and an event $A\subseteq\Omega$, we use $\mathcal{F}\cap A$ to denote the $\sigma$-algebra consisting of the intersections of the events in $\mathcal{F}$ with $A$. We start to prove that for each $m\leq m'$, every event in the $\sigma$-algebra
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unchanged sigma algebra}
\mathcal{F}_{m'}\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\}    
\end{equation}
remains unchanged after the permutation. First, it follows from definitions that
\begin{equation}
\{k_{m}\geq k\}=\big\{\min_{0\leq j\leq T_{k}}\mathcal{L}(j)> -m\big\}.    
\end{equation} 
This event remains unchanged after the permutation since permuting $X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{k}$ does not change $T_{k}$ and $\min_{0\leq j\leq T_{k}}\mathcal{L}(j)$. Second, since $k_{m'}\geq k_{m}$ when $m'\geq m$, on the event $\{k_{m}\geq k\}$, $\mathcal{Z}|_{[T_{k_{m'}},\infty)}$ remains unchanged after the permutation. Finally, \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex} implies that each directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{T_{k_{m'}}}$ has to visit $u_{T_{j}},0\leq j\leq k_{m'}$ exactly once, and its part from $u_{T_{j-1}}$ to $u_{T_{j}}$ is completely contained in $(\mathfrak{m}_{T_{j-1},T_{j}},\vv{E}_{T_{j-1},T_{j}})$ for all $1\leq j\leq k_{m'}$. So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length of sdp summable}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m'}}})=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{m'}}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{T_{j-1}},u_{T_{j}}),    
\end{equation}
and for every $1\leq j\leq k_{m'}$, the $j$-th term in the summation on the right hand side is determined by $X_{j}$ via the Mullin bijection in \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection}. Since $k_{m'}\geq k_{m}$ when $m'\geq m$, this implies that on the event $\{k_{m}\geq k\}$, $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(u_{0},u_{T_{k_{m'}}})$ remains unchanged after the permutation as the right hand side of \eqref{eq:length of sdp summable} does. That every event in $\mathcal{F}_{m'}\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\}$ remains unchanged after the permutation when $m'\geq m$ then follows from the definition of $\mathcal{F}_{m'}$ in \eqref{eq:def of sigma algebra}.

Now as $\mathcal{F}_{m}$ is decreasing in $m$, it follows from the above paragraph that for each $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, every event in the $\sigma$-algebra
\begin{equation}\label{intersection of unchanged sigma algebra}
\bigcap_{m'\geq m}\Big(\mathcal{F}_{m'}\cap \{k_{m}\geq k\}\Big)=\Big(\bigcap_{m'\geq m}\mathcal{F}_{m'}\Big)\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\}=\Big(\bigcap_{m'\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{m'}\Big)\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\}     
\end{equation}
remains unchanged after the permutation. So for any event $E$ in $\cap_{m'\geq 1}\mathcal{F}_{m'}$ and any $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, the event $E\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\}$ also remains unchanged after the permutation. Meanwhile, we know that $k_{m}$ almost surely increases to $\infty$ as $m$ goes to $\infty$. The conclusion thus follows since every event $E$ can be written as the increasing limit of $E\cap\{k_{m}\geq k\},m\geq 1$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Recall that for each $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we use $x_{-k}$ (resp.\ $y_{k}$) to denote the $(k+1)$-th vertex on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ (resp.\ $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$). 

For each $s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we define $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$) as the directed submap of $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})$) in the following way. For $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$, find the smallest $k\geq s$ such that $x_{-k}$ lies on $P_{0}$ (such $x_{-k}$ exists since there are infinitely many boundary cut vertices). We take $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ as the intersection of $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L})$ with the unique bounded closed region bounded by the counterclockwise arc from $x_{0}$ to $x_{-k}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ and the part of $P_{0}$ from $x_{0}$ to $x_{-k}$. For $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$, find the smallest $k\geq s$ such that $y_{k}$ is connected to some vertex on $P_{0}$ by a green edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ (such $y_{k}$ exists again since there are infinitely many boundary cut vertices). Take $x$ as the closest vertex to $x_{0}$ on $P_{0}$ such that $y_{k}$ is connected to $x$ by a green edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. We take $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$ as the intersection of $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})$ with the unique bounded closed region bounded by the clockwise arc from $x_{0}$ to $y_{k}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, the green edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ connecting $x$ to $y_{k}$, and the part of $P_{0}$ from $x_{0}$ to $x$. See \Cref{fig:last proof} for an illustration.

Fix $s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. For every $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, define the event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of Em}
E_{m}=E_m(s):=\Big\{\min\big\{-\mathcal{L}(T_{k_{m}}),-\mathcal{R}(T_{k_{m}})\big\}\geq s\Big\}.    
\end{equation}
This event is measurable with respect to $\mathcal{Z}(T_{k_{m}})$ and thus belongs to the $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{F}_{m}$. Recall the definition of $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R})$) above \Cref{lem:conditional independence in Boltzmann beads}. Then on the event $E_{m}$, the map $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$) is a directed submap of $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R})$) as the smallest $k\geq s$ in the last paragraph is at most $k_{m}$ (because on $E_m$ we have $-\mathcal{L}(T_{k_{m}})\geq s$ and $-\mathcal{R}(T_{k_{m}})\geq s$). Furthermore, in this case $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$) is determined by $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,T_{k_{m}}}^{R})$) by construction.

Now it follows from \Cref{lem:conditional independence in Boltzmann beads} and the last paragraph that for every $m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, on the $\mathcal{F}_{m}$-measurable event $E_{m}$, the maps $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$ are conditionally independent given $\mathcal{F}_{m}$. Thus, for every bounded measurable functional $f_L$ of directed maps
and every bounded measurable functional $f_R$ of directed maps, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:XL and XR}
X^L:=f_L(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})
\quad\text{and}\quad
X^R:=f_R(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R}).
\end{equation}
For every
$m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we then have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional independence of XL and XR}
\mathbb{E}[X^LX^R\mid\mathcal{F}_m]\mathbbm 1_{E_m}
=
\mathbb{E}[X^L\mid\mathcal{F}_m]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X^R\mid\mathcal{F}_m]\mathbbm 1_{E_m}.
\end{equation}
We then take $m$ to $\infty$. It follows from \Cref{lem:monotonicity and tail triviality} and the backward martingale convergence theorem that all the conditional expectations converge to unconditional expectations, and it follows from the unboundedness of $\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}$  that $\mathbbm{1}_{E_{m}}$ increases to $1$ almost surely. In all, taking the limit yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale convergence}
\mathbb{E}[X^{L}X^{R}]=\mathbb{E}[X^{L}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[X^{R}].
\end{equation}
Since $f_{L}$ and $f_{R}$ are arbitrary, we obtain that $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ and $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$ are independent.

Finally, sending $s$ to $\infty$ concludes the proof as $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{L},\vv{E}_{s}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{s}^{R},\vv{E}_{s}^{R})$) increases to $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{L},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{L})$ (resp.\ $(\mathsf{M}_{0,\infty}^{R},\vv{E}_{0,\infty}^{R})$) when $s$ goes to infinity.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:express Bu by Bu1}
Depending on the value of $\mathcal{Z}(1)$, the Busemann functions $\mathcal{X}^{1}$ and $\mathcal{X}$ satisfy the following relations almost surely.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{relation:(-1,0)} If $\mathcal{Z}(1)$ is $(-1,0)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relation for (-1,0)}
\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\Big(-1,\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\Big).    
\end{equation}
\item\label{relation:(0,-1)} If $\mathcal{Z}(1)$ is $(0,-1)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relation for (0,-1)}
\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\Big(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),\ 0\Big).    
\end{equation}
\item\label{relation:(1,0)} If $\mathcal{Z}(1)$ is $(1,0)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relation for (1,0)}
\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\Big(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\},\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\Big).    
\end{equation}
\item\label{relation:(0,1)} If $\mathcal{Z}(1)$ is $(0,1)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relation for (0,1)}
\Big(\mathcal{X}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=\Big(\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\},\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\}\Big).    
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Recall from around \Cref{def:FUIST map} the sequence of vertices $\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ with respect to which we define the Busemann function, and that for each $k\geq 0$, the vertex $x_{-k}$ (resp.\ $y_{k}$) is the $(k+1)$-th vertex on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ (resp.\ $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$) when counting from the red (resp.\ blue) endpoint of the starting edge $\lambda_{0}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. Also recall from \Cref{def:infinite directed geodesic} and \Cref{cor:infinite directed geodesic} that for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, $P_{k}$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. For each $k\geq 0$, we use $x_{-k}^{1},x_{k}^{1},y_{k}^{1}$ to denote the counterpart vertices of $x_{-k},x_{k},y_{k}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{1,\infty}$, and use $P_{-k}^{1},P_{k}^{1}$ to denote the counterpart leftmost infinite directed geodesics of $P_{-k},P_{k}$ in $(\mathfrak{m}_{1,\infty},\vv{E}_{1,\infty})$. (The existence of these objects again follows from the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$.) For the following argument, see \Cref{fig:recursive relation} for an illustration. 
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{express proof:first case}
\underline{\textbf{In the first case~\ref{relation:(-1,0)}}}, the step $\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(-1,0)$ corresponds via the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} to a red triangle in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ with red vertices $x_{-1},x_{0}$ and blue vertex $y_{0}$. Then writing $\vv{e}$ as the directed red edge on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ from $x_{0}$ to $x_{-1}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vertex and edge correspondence in (-1,0)}
x_{-1} = x_{0}^{1},\quad x_{1}=x_{1}^{1},\quad\text{and}\quad \vv{E}_{0,\infty} = \vv{E}_{1,\infty} \cup \{\vv{e}\}.
\end{equation}
In particular, the vertex $x_{-1}$ is a boundary cut vertex by \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex}, and $P_{0}$ has to pass this vertex by \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}. Therefore $P_{-1}$ is the same as $P_{0}^{1}$; $P_{0}$ is the directed edge $\vv{e}$ followed by $P_{0}^{1}$; and $P_{1}$ is the same as $P_{1}^{1}$. Applying \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} and normalizing $\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)$ as $0$ then yields \eqref{eq:relation for (-1,0)}.

\item\label{express proof:second case} \underline{\textbf{In the second case~\ref{relation:(0,-1)}}}, the step $\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,-1)$ corresponds via the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} to a blue triangle in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ with red vertex $x_{0}$ and blue vertices $y_{0}$ and $y_{1}$. Then we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vertex and edge correspondence in (0,-1)}
x_{-1} = x_{-1}^{1},\quad x_{0}=x_{0}^{1},\quad x_{1}=x_{0}^{1},\quad\text{and}\quad\vv{E}_{0,\infty}=\vv{E}_{1,\infty}.
\end{equation}
Therefore $P_{-1}$ is the same as $P_{-1}^{1}$, and both $P_{0}$ and $P_{1}$ are the same as $P_{0}^{1}$. Applying \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} and normalizing $\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)$ as $0$ then yields \eqref{eq:relation for (0,-1)}. 

\item\label{express proof:third case} \underline{\textbf{In the third case~\ref{relation:(1,0)}}}, the step $\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(1,0)$ corresponds via the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} to a red triangle in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ with red vertices $x_{0}$ and $x_{0}^{1}$ and blue vertex $y_{0}$, where $x_{0}^{1}$ is not on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. Then writing $\vv{e}$ as the directed red edge on this triangle from $x_{0}$ to $x_{0}^{1}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vertex and edge correspondence in (1,0)}
x_{-1} = x_{-2}^{1},\quad x_{0}=x_{-1}^{1},\quad x_{1}=x_{1}^{1},\quad\text{and}\quad \vv{E}_{0,\infty} = \vv{E}_{1,\infty}\cup\{\vv{e}\}.
\end{equation}
(We stress that the directed edge that reverses $\vv{e}$ and points from $x_{0}^{1}$ to $x_{0}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{1,\infty}$.) Recall the definitions of $\tau_{1}$ and $\eta_{1}$ in \eqref{eq:def of tauk and etak}. Clearly $\tau_{1}>1$ and $\eta_{1}>1$. As a result, it follows from \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap} that both $P_{-1}$ and $P_{1}$ are infinite directed geodesics in $(\mathfrak{m}_{1,\infty},\vv{E}_{1,\infty})$. Thus $P_{-1}$ is the same as $P_{-2}^{1}$ and $P_{1}$ is the same as $P_{1}^{1}$. In addition, $P_{0}$ is $P_{-1}^{1}$ or the directed edge $\vv{e}$ followed by $P_{0}^{1}$, depending on which one is shorter up to the vertex where they coalesce as in \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic}. This is because $P_{0}$ does not visit a vertex twice and these two choices are respectively the best choice for not using or using $\vv{e}$ in $P_{0}$. Applying \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} and normalizing $\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)$ as $0$ then yields \eqref{eq:relation for (1,0)}.

\item\label{express proof:fourth case} \underline{\textbf{In the fourth case~\ref{relation:(0,1)}}}, the step $\mathcal{Z}(1)-\mathcal{Z}(0)=(0,1)$ corresponds via the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} to a blue triangle in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ with red vertex $x_{0}$ and blue vertices $y_{0},y_{0}^{1}$, where $y_{0}^{1}$ is not on $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. Then writing $\vv{e}$ as the unique directed black edge in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ that crosses the blue edge on this triangle connecting $y_{0}$ with $y_{0}^{1}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vertex and edge correspondence in (0,1)}
x_{-1} = x_{-1}^{1},\quad x_{0}=x_{0}^{1},\quad x_{1}=x_{2}^{1},\quad\text{and}\quad\vv{E}_{0,\infty} = \vv{E}_{1,\infty}\cup\{\vv{e}\}.
\end{equation}
Recall the definitions of $\tau_{1}$ and $\eta_{1}$ in \eqref{eq:def of tauk and etak}. Clearly $\tau_{1}>1$ and $\eta_{1}>1$. As a result, it follows from \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap} that both $P_{-1}$ and $P_{1}$ are infinite directed geodesics in $(\mathfrak{m}_{1,\infty},\vv{E}_{1,\infty})$. Thus $P_{-1}$ is the same as $P_{-1}^{1}$ and $P_{1}$ is the same as $P_{2}^{1}$. In addition, $P_{0}$ is $P_{0}^{1}$ or the directed edge $\vv{e}$ followed by $P_{1}^{1}$, depending on which one is shorter up to the vertex where they coalesce as in \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic}. This is because $P_{0}$ does not visit a vertex twice and these two choices are respectively the best choice for not using or using $\vv{e}$ in $P_{0}$. Applying \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} and normalizing $\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)$ as $0$ then yields \eqref{eq:relation for (0,1)}.\qedhere
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:recursive relation on f and g}
For all $x\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $y\in\mathbb{Z}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:recursive relation on f and g}
\begin{split}
4f(x)g(y)=&\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{x=1\}}g(y+1)+f(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\{y=0\}}+\\
&f(x)\sum_{z=0}^{1}f(z)g(y-z)+g(y+1)\sum_{z\leq -1}f(z)f(x-z-1)+\\
&g(y+1)\sum_{z\leq -1}g(z)f(x-z-1)+f(x)\sum_{z\geq 0}g(z)g(y-z).  
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
First, we know from the deterministic bounds \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:bound for negative} that $f(x)$ is $0$ for all $x>1$. Thus the equation \eqref{eq:recursive relation on f and g} holds when $x>1$ since all the terms in both sides are zero. Now suppose $x\leq 1$. Because $\mathcal{X}^{1}$ has the same law as $\mathcal{X}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:same law}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}(1)\Big)=(x,y)\Big]= \mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)\Big)=(x,y)\Big].   
\end{equation}
Plugging in \Cref{lem:express Bu by Bu1} and using that $\mathcal{Z}$ has the law of a simple random walk, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:probability the same}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ 4\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)\Big)=(x,y)\Big]\\ 
&=\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(1,\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1\Big)=(x,y)\Big]+\\
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),\ 0\Big)=(x,y)\Big]+\\
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)+\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\},\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}\Big)=(x,y)\Big]+\\
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[\Big(-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)+\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\},\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\}\Big)=(x,y)\Big].
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Again using that $\mathcal{X}^{1}$ has the same law as $\mathcal{X}$, it follows from the stationarity \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:stationarity} and definitions of $f$ and $g$ in \eqref{eq:def of f and g} that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:f and g for every k}
\begin{split}
& f(x)=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)=x],\quad\text{for every }k\leq 0,\quad\text{and}\\
& g(y)=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}^{1}(k)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(k-1)=y],\quad\text{for every }k\geq 1.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Plugging \eqref{eq:f and g for every k} into \eqref{eq:probability the same} and then using the independence property \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:independence} as well as the fact that $\mathcal{X}^{1}(0)$ is $0$ yields \eqref{eq:recursive relation on f and g}, where the $1$st, $2$nd, $3$rd and $4$th terms on the right hand side of \eqref{eq:probability the same} respectively give the $1$st, $2$nd, $3$rd and $4$th, and $5$th and $6$th terms on the right hand side of \eqref{eq:recursive relation on f and g}. 

Indeed, the correspondence for the first two terms is clear. For the third term, because $\mathcal{X}$ and $\mathcal{X}^{1}$ are equally distributed, it follows from \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:bound for negative} that $\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)$ is at least $-1$. We use the variable $z$ to represent the value of $-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)$, which can only be an integer that is at most $1$. When $-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=z$ is $0$ or $1$, we have $\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}=\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)$. In this case 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:third term first sum}
\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)=x\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=y\ \implies\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y-z,    
\end{equation}
leading to the first summation. When $-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=z$ is at most $-1$,  we have $\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1),1\}=1$. In this case
\begin{equation}\label{eq:third term second sum}
-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)=x-1\ \implies\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-2)=x-z-1\quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y+1,    
\end{equation}
leading to the second summation. For the fourth term, use the variable $z$ to represent the value of $\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)$, which can be any integer a priori. When $\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=z$ is at most $-1$, we have $\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\}=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1$. In this case
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fourth term first sum}
\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=x-1\ \implies\ -\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=x-z-1\quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y+1,
\end{equation}
leading to the first summation. When $\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=z$ is at least $0$, we have $\min\{\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)+1,0\}=0$. In this case \begin{equation}\label{eq:fourth term second sum}
-\mathcal{X}^{1}(-1)=x\quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{X}^{1}(2)=y\ \implies\ \mathcal{X}^{1}(2)-\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)=y-z,
\end{equation}
leading to the second summation.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:finite mean}
Both $|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|$ and $|\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)|$ have finite mean.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{We first prove that $|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|$ has a finite mean.}} Assume the opposite. In light of the fact that the deterministic bounds \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:bound for negative} imply $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\leq 1$, we must have \begin{equation}\label{eq:expectation is infinity 1}
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\big]=-\infty.   \end{equation}
In addition, \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:independence} and \ref{property:stationarity} tell us that the increments $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1),k\leq 0,$ are i.i.d. So, as a result of the law of large numbers, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:law of large numbers 1}
\frac{\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-k)}{k}\overset{\text{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow} -\infty\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.   
\end{equation}
However, \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}, \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex} and \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time} imply that there is an infinite sequence of distinct boundary cut vertices $(x_{-k_{n}})_{n\geq 1}$ of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. By \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, each of these boundary cut vertices has to be visited by the leftmost infinite directed geodesic $P_{0}$ in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{0}$ to $\infty$. It then follows from \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} that $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-k_{n})$ is non-negative for every $n\geq 1$, a contradiction to \eqref{eq:law of large numbers 1}. So $|\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)|$ has a finite mean.

\underline{\textbf{We then prove that $|\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)|$ has a finite mean.}} Assume the opposite. In light of the fact that \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:exponential negative tail for positive} says the distribution of the negative part of $\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)$ has an exponential tail, we must have \begin{equation}\label{eq:expectation is infinity 2}
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\big]=\infty.    
\end{equation}
Now \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:independence} and \ref{property:stationarity} tell us that the increments $\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(k-1),k\geq 1$ are i.i.d. So as a result of the law of large numbers, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:law of large numbers 2}
\frac{\mathcal{X}(k)-\mathcal{X}(0)}{k}\overset{\text{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow} \infty\quad\text{as }k\to\infty.   
\end{equation}
However, \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time} implies that there is an infinite sequence of simultaneous running minimum times $T_{n},n\geq 1$ that satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:special cone time}
\mathcal{R}(T_{n})=\min_{0\leq j\leq T_{n}-1}\mathcal{R}(j)-1,\quad\text{for every }n\geq 1.    
\end{equation}
By \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} and \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex}, the vertices $u_{T_{n}},n\geq 1$ are boundary cut vertices of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$. By \Cref{lem:boundary cut vertex}, each of these boundary cut vertices has to be visited by the leftmost infinite directed geodesic $P_{0}$ in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{0}$ to $\infty$. Meanwhile,  \Cref{def:cone time} and \eqref{eq:special cone time} imply that for every $n\geq 1$, $T_{n}$ equals $\eta_{-\mathcal{R}(T_{n})}$ (recall \eqref{eq:def of tauk and etak}). This means that for each $n\geq 1$, the boundary cut vertex $u_{T_{n}}$ is the same as the vertex $x_{-\mathcal{R}(T_{n})}$. Combining the above facts, it then follows from \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} that $\mathcal{X}\big(-\mathcal{R}(T_{n})\big)-\mathcal{X}\big(0\big)$ is non-positive for every $n\geq 1$. As $-\mathcal{R}(T_{n})$ tends to $\infty$, this is a contradiction to \eqref{eq:law of large numbers 2}. So $|\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)|$ has finite mean.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:first and second parameter 0}
We have $r_{1}=r_{2}=0$. In addition, both $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)$ and $\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)$ have mean~$0$.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Combining \Cref{lem:finite mean} with \cite[Theorem 3.3.18]{Dur19} implies that $F(t)$ and $G(s)$ are differentiable at $0$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F and G differentiable}
F'(0)=i\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\big]\quad\text{and}\quad G'(0)=i\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\big].    
\end{equation}
This means 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F and G first order expansion}
F(t) = 1+it\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\big]+o(t)\quad\text{and}\quad G(s) = 1+is\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\big]+o(s).    
\end{equation}
So as a result of \eqref{eq:symmetry equation again}, we have $r_{1}=0$ and further 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2 positive and negative}
-\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)\big]^{2}=-r_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad -\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)\big]^{2}=r_{2}. \end{equation}
Therefore, $r_{2}\geq 0$ and $r_{2}\leq 0$ and hence $r_{2}=0$. Combining this with \eqref{eq:r2 positive and negative} then further implies that both $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)$ and $\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)$ have mean $0$.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:third parameter not 0}
We have $r_{3}\neq 0$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Suppose $r_{3}=0$. Recall that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of R again}
R(t)= e^{-it}c(0)-c(t)-e^{-it}G^{-}(0)+G^{-}(t)-2e^{-it}+2,   
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of c and G- again}
c(t)=f(0)+f(1)e^{it}\quad\text{and}\quad G^{-}(s)=\sum_{y\leq -1}g(y)e^{isy}.    
\end{equation}
Doing a Taylor expansion of the above two equations in a small neighborhood of $t=0$ and comparing the resulting expression to \eqref{eq:Taylor expansion for R} gives 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r1 and r3 expression}
\begin{split}
r_{1}&\ =-f(0)-2f(1)+\sum_{y\leq -1}g(y)(1+y)+2,\quad\text{and}\\
6r_{3}&\ =f(0)+2f(1)-\sum_{y\leq -1}g(y)(1+y^{3})-2.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Since we have proved in \Cref{lem:first and second parameter 0} that $r_{1}$ is $0$ and have assumed that $r_{3}$ is $0$, summing the two equations in \eqref{eq:r1 and r3 expression} then gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r3 expression again}
\sum_{y\leq -1}g(y)(y-y^{3})=0.   
\end{equation}
Now $g$ is non-negative, and $y-y^{3}$ is $0$ when $y=-1$ and strictly positive when $y\leq -2$, so (recall that we have normalized so that $\mathcal{X}(0)$ is $0$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:g is 0 when smaller than -2}
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)=y]=g(y)=0,\quad\text{for all }y\leq -2.   
\end{equation}

For every $k\geq 0$, because $(\mathfrak{m}_{k,\infty},\vv{E}_{k,\infty})$ has the same law as $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ by the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$, we can define the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}^{k}$ on $(\mathfrak{m}_{k,\infty},\vv{E}_{k,\infty})$, which has the same law as $\mathcal{X}$. For every $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, define the event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of Ek}
E_{k}:=\Big\{\mathcal{Z}(j)-\mathcal{Z}(j-1)=(-1,0),\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq k\Big\}.    
\end{equation}
Then $\mathbb{P}[E_{k}]=1/4^{k}$ for all $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. In addition, again using the stationarity of both the simple random walk $\mathcal{Z}$ and the Busemann functions, it follows from \Cref{lem:express Bu by Bu1}\ref{relation:(-1,0)} that for every $k\geq 1$, on the event $E_{k}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann change k steps}
\mathcal{X}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{j}(1)-j\quad\text{for all }1\leq j\leq k.
\end{equation}
So combining \eqref{eq:g is 0 when smaller than -2} and \eqref{eq:Busemann change k steps}, we finally obtain (here we use the Markov property of $\mathcal{Z}$ in the first equality, and use the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$ in the second equality)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann bounded from below}
\begin{split}
0&\geq \mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)\leq -2]\geq \mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}^{k}(1)\leq k-2,E_{k}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}^{k}(1)\leq k-2]\cdot\mathbb{P}[E_{k}]\\
&=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)\leq k-2]/4^{k},\quad\text{for every }k\geq 1. 
\end{split}
\end{equation}
So $\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)\leq k-2]$ equals $0$ for all $k\geq 1$, which is clearly impossible. So $r_{3}$ must be non-zero.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
The fact that both $\mathcal{X}(1)-\mathcal{X}(0)$ and $\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-1)$ have mean $0$ follows from \Cref{lem:first and second parameter 0}. We then move on to the tail estimates. From \Cref{lem:first and second parameter 0,lem:third parameter not 0} we know that $r_{1}$ and $r_{2}$ are zero, while $r_{3}$ is not. Therefore, \eqref{eq:Taylor expansion for R} gives 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:expansion of R}
R(t)=ir_{3}t^{3}+o(t^{3})\text{ as }t\to 0,\quad\text{where }r_{3}\in\mathbb{R}.
