\end{equation}
Because $F(t)$ and $G(s)$ are characteristic functions, $F(t)$ is continuous in $t\in\mathbb{R}$, $G(s)$ is continuous in $s\in\mathbb{R}$, $F(-t)=\overline{F}(t)$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$, and $G(-s)=\overline{G}(s)$ for all $s\in\mathbb{R}$. So it follows from \eqref{eq:symmetry equation again} and \eqref{eq:expansion of R} that there exist constants $w_{F},w_{G}\in\mathbb{C}$
such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F and G expansion wF and wG}
\begin{cases}
F(t)=1+(w_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}+\overline{w_{F}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t<0\}})|t|^{3/2}+o(|t|^{3/2})\text{ as }t\to 0\quad\text{where}\quad w_{F}^{2}=-\overline{w_{F}}^{2}=-ir_{3}\neq 0,\\
G(s)=1+(w_{G}\mathbbm{1}_{\{s>0\}}+\overline{w_{G}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{s<0\}})|s|^{3/2}+o(|s|^{3/2})\text{ as }s\to 0\quad\text{where}\quad w_{G}^{2}=-\overline{w_{G}}^{2}=ir_{3}\neq 0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
As $r_{3}\in\mathbb{R}$, this implies (the $\pm$ means that there are two possible cases for $+$ and $-$, similar below)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wF and wG}
w_{G}=\pm \overline{w_{F}}\quad\text{and}\quad |\mathrm{Re}(w_{F})|=|\mathrm{Im}(w_{F})|\neq 0.
\end{equation} 

Recall that we have set $\mathcal{X}(0)=0$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of pG}
p_{G}^{-}:=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)\leq -1]\quad\implies\quad 1-p_{G}^{-}=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{X}(1)\geq 0].  
\end{equation} 
Then the deterministic bounds \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:bound for negative} say $-\mathcal{X}(-1)\leq 1$. In addition, recall the definition of $G^{-}(s),s\in\mathbb{R}$ in \eqref{eq:def of c and G-}. Then $G^{-}(s)+1-p_{G}^{-}$ is the characteristic function of $\mathcal{X}(1)\mathbbm{1}\big\{\mathcal{X}(1)\leq -1\big\}$. So the exponential tail \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann}\ref{property:exponential negative tail for positive} implies that $G^{-}(s)$ is smooth in a neighbourhood of $s=0$, and that we can define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of c-}
w_{G}^{-}=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)\mathbbm{1}\big\{\mathcal{X}(1)\leq -1\big\}\big]\leq 0.    
\end{equation}
Given this, it follows from \cite[Theorem 3.3.18]{Dur19} that the coefficient of $s$ in the Taylor expansion of $G^{-}(s)$ in a small neighbourhood of $s=0$ is equal to $iw_{G}^{-}$. We claim that $w_{G}^{-}$ is strictly negative. Indeed, $G(s)$ is the characteristic function of $\mathcal{X}(1)$, so by \Cref{lem:finite mean}, \eqref{eq:F and G expansion wF and wG} and \cite[Theorem 3.3.18]{Dur19} again, $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(1)\big]=0$. Therefore, if $w_{G}^{-}=0$, then $\mathcal{X}(1)$ almost surely equals $0$. This cannot hold since $\mathcal{X}^{1}$ has the same distribution as $\mathcal{X}$ by the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$, and there is a positive chance that $\mathcal{X}(1)=\mathcal{X}^{1}(1)-1$ by \Cref{lem:express Bu by Bu1}\ref{relation:(-1,0)}. So $w_{G}^{-}<0$.

Now the characteristic functions of the non-negative integer-valued random variables $\mathcal{X}(-1)+1$ and $\mathcal{X}(1)\mathbbm{1}\big\{\mathcal{X}(1)\geq 0\big\}$ are respectively $e^{it}F(-t)$ and $G(s)-G^{-}(s)+p_{G}^{-}$. In addition, \eqref{eq:F and G expansion wF and wG} implies that (the $\pm$ means that there are two possible cases for $+$ and $-$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F and G expansion variation}
\begin{cases}
e^{it}F(-t)=1+it+(\overline{w_{F}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>0\}}+w_{F}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t<0\}})|t|^{3/2}+o(|t|^{3/2})\quad\text{as }t\to 0,\\
G(s)-G^{-}(s)+p_{G}^{-}=1-iw_{G}^{-}s+\big(w_{G}\mathbbm{1}_{\{s>0\}}+\overline{w_{G}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{s<0\}}\big)|s|^{3/2}+o(|s|^{3/2})\quad\text{as }s\to 0. 
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Furthermore, we have proved $w_{G}^{-}<0$ in the last paragraph, and have proved $\overline{w_{F}},w_{G}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus \mathbb{R}$ in \eqref{eq:wF and wG}. The conclusion then follows from applying \cite[Proposition 6.1 Case 2]{BG25} on the non-negative integer-valued random variables $\mathcal{X}(-1)+1$ and $\mathcal{X}(1)\mathbbm{1}\big\{\mathcal{X}(1)\geq 0\big\}$. (In particular, \cite[Proposition 6.1 Case 2]{BG25} implies that $\mathrm{Im}(\overline{w_{F}})=\mathrm{Im}(w_{G})<0$, so we should choose $+$ in the $\pm$ of \eqref{eq:wF and wG}.)
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:uniform upper bound Mullin map}
There exist positive constants $c_{3}$ and $c_{4}$ such that for all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$, it holds for the directed uniform Mullin map $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ of size n\footnote{It follows from the Mullin bijection in \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection} that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ is a directed uniform Mullin map of size $n$.} that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform upper bound Mullin map}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]\leq c_{3} \exp(-c_{4} C^{3/4}).    
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{Basic setup.}} For a finite Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$, recall the definitions of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}$, $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}$ given above \Cref{def:Mullin map to walk}. Also recall that for $D\in \{\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}\}$, we use $\# D$ to denote the number of edges or half edges in $D$. We further write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:NL and NR}
N_{L}(\mathfrak{m})=1+\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}+\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}\quad\text{and}\quad N_{R}(\mathfrak{m})=1+\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}+\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}.    
\end{equation}
Then both $N_{L}(\mathfrak{m})$ and $N_{R}(\mathfrak{m})$ only depend on $(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2})$, because $\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}+\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}$ is equal to the number of edges in the clockwise arc $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$ of $\partial\overline{M}$ from the apex $x^{1}$ of $C^{1}$ to the apex $x^{2}$ of $C^{2}$, and $\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}+\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}$ is equal to the number of half edges in $\overline{M}$. In addition, in the definition of Mullin map in \Cref{def:Mullin map}, the marked vertex $\overline{v}$ is allowed to be an arbitrary vertex of $\overline{M}$ on $\partial\overline{M}(C_{1},C_{2})$, and the marked half edge $\overline{e}$ is allowed to be empty (denoted by $\emptyset$) or an arbitrary half edge of $\overline{M}$. This means that for each Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}=(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2},\overline{v},\overline{e})$, there are in total $N_{L}(\mathfrak{m})\times N_{R}(\mathfrak{m})$ Mullin maps that share the same first four coordinates $(\overline{M},T,C^{1},C^{2})$ with $\mathfrak{m}$.

Fix arbitrary $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and $\widetilde{C}>0$. Define the event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of E distance too large}
E_{k}=E_{k}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}):=\left\{\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}}(u_{0},u_{k})\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3}\right\}.   \end{equation} 
Note that if we expand $\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}=(\overline{M}_{0,k},T_{0,k},C^{1}_{0,k},C^{2}_{0,k},\overline{v}_{0,k},\overline{e}_{0,k})$ as in \Cref{def:Mullin map}, then $u_{0}$ is exactly the apex of $C^{1}_{0,k}$, and $u_{k}$ is exactly the apex of $C^{2}_{0,k}$. 

Further write $\mathcal{M}_{k}$ as the set of Mullin maps $\mathfrak{m}_{k}$ of size $k$, and write $\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$ as the set of Mullin maps $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}$ of size $k$ such that $\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}=\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}=0$. Then we can define a mapping $\phi$ from $\mathcal{M}_{k}$ to $\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$ in the following way: For each element $\mathfrak{m}_{k}=(\overline{M}_{k},T_{k},C^{1}_{k},C^{2}_{k},\overline{v}_{k},\overline{e}_{k})$ in $\mathcal{M}_{k}$, we let $\phi(\mathfrak{m}_{k})$ be the Mullin map $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}=(\overline{M}_{k},T_{k},C^{1}_{k},C^{2}_{k},x^{2}_{k},\emptyset)$ where $x^{2}_{k}$ is the apex of $C^{2}_{k}$. By the last paragraph, each element $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}$ in $\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$ has $N_{L}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})\times N_{R}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})$ pre-images in $\mathcal{M}_{k}$. In addition, for each $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$, all these pre-images share the same orientation set $\vv{E}_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}}$ with $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}$ by \Cref{lem:uniform marked point}. Then since we already know $\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}$ is uniform in $\mathcal{M}_{k}$, it follows from the above properties that  
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prob of E}
\mathbb{P}[E_{k}]=\Big(\sum_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{k}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})}\cdot N_{L}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})\cdot N_{R}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})\Big)\Big/\Big(\sum_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}}N_{L}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})\cdot N_{R}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})\Big).   
\end{equation}
Clearly $N_{L}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})$ and $N_{R}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k})$ are at least $1$ for each $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$. On the other hand, for an arbitrary $\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}$, let $\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}},\widetilde{\mathcal{R}})$ be its encoding nearest-neighbour walk on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ started from $(0,0)$ via the Mullin bijection. Then we know from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:NL and NR bound}
N_{L}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}) = 1+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(k)-2\min_{0\leq j\leq k}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(j)\leq 4k\quad\text{and}\quad N_{R}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}) = 1+\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(k)-2\min_{0\leq j\leq k}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(j)\leq 4k.    
\end{equation}
Plugging these bounds into \eqref{eq:prob of E} then yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prob of E conditioning}
\mathbb{P}[E_{k}]\leq \Big(\sum_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{k}}\cdot 4k\cdot 4k\Big)\Big/\Big(\sum_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}}1\Big)=16k^{2}\mathbb{P}\Big[E_{k}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}\Big].    
\end{equation}
By \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} and \Cref{def:cone time}, the event $\{\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\mathrm{cut}}\}$ is equivalent to the event $\{k\in S\}$, where $S$ is the set of simultaneous running minimum times defined in \Cref{def:cone time}. Since \eqref{eq:prob that n in S} implies that the latter event has probability at least $c/k$, it then follows that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:remove the conditioning}
\mathbb{P}[E_{k}]\leq 16k^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[E_{k}\mid k\in S]=\frac{16k^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S]}{\mathbb{P}[k\in S]}\leq \frac{16k^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S]}{c/k}=\frac{k^{3}}{c'}\cdot\mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S].   \end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{We now bound $\mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S]$ from above.}} By definition, on the event $\{k\in S\}$, we have $u_{k}=x_{-\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}}$. Now, \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} implies that on the event $\{k\in S\}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boundary and walk}
0=\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}=\mathcal{L}(k)-\min_{0\leq j\leq k}\mathcal{L}(j)\quad\implies\quad \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}=-\min_{0\leq j\leq k}\mathcal{L}(j)=-\mathcal{L}(k).   
\end{equation}
So on the event $\{k\in S\}$, we have $u_{k}=x_{\mathcal{L}(k)}$, and we further know that this vertex is a boundary cut vertex of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$ by \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex}. Since we always have $u_{0}=x_{0}$, combining \Cref{cor:two parts of leftmost infinite directed geodesic,cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} then gives that on the event $\{k\in S\}$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:result of n in S}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}}(u_{0},u_{k})=\mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}\big(\mathcal{L}(k)\big)=-\mathcal{X}\big(\mathcal{L}(k)\big),    
\end{equation}
and thus 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:separate into two parts}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S]=\mathbb{P}\Big[-\mathcal{X}\big(\mathcal{L}(k)\big)\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3},k\in S\Big]\\
&\leq \mathbb{P}\Big[\mathcal{L}(k)\leq -\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k}\Big] + \mathbb{P}\Big[0\geq \mathcal{L}(k)\geq -\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k},-\mathcal{X}\big(\mathcal{L}(k)\big)\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3}\Big].   
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Because $\mathcal{L}(k)$ is a sum of $k$ i.i.d.~random variables bounded in $[-1,1]$, it follows from Hoeffding's inequality that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hoeffding}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathcal{L}(k)\leq -\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k}\Big]\leq \exp\Big(-2\big(\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k}\big)^{2}/4k\Big) = \exp\Big(-\widetilde{C}^{3/4}/2\Big).    
\end{equation}
In addition, it follows from \Cref{thm:properties of Busemann,thm:tail estimate} that $\mathcal{X}(j)-\mathcal{X}(j-1),j\leq 0,$ are i.i.d.~mean $0$ random variables, each of which is bounded from above by $1$ and has tail decay of order $x^{-3/2}$. Applying a basic concentration bound for sums of i.i.d.~random variables which are bounded above (e.g.~\cite[Lemma 5.4]{GM21b}) then yields that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:strong concentration}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[0\geq \mathcal{L}(k)\geq -\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k},-\mathcal{X}\big(\mathcal{L}(k)\big)\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3}\Big]\leq \mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq j\leq \widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k}}-\mathcal{X}(-j)\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3}\Big]\\
&=\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq j\leq \widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k}}-\mathcal{X}(-j)\geq \widetilde{C}^{3/4}\cdot(\widetilde{C}^{3/8}\sqrt{k})^{2/3}\Big]\leq c\exp\Big(-c'\widetilde{C}^{3/4}\Big).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:separate into two parts}-\eqref{eq:strong concentration} then gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fix n concentration}
\mathbb{P}[E_{k},k\in S]\leq c\exp\Big(-c'\widetilde{C}^{3/4}\Big). 
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{Conclusion.}} Plugging the above equation into \eqref{eq:remove the conditioning} and using the definition of $E_{k}$ in \eqref{eq:def of E distance too large}, we thus obtain that for any $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and $\widetilde{C}>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:final bound}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,k}}(u_{0},u_{k})\geq \widetilde{C}k^{1/3}\Big]\leq ck^{3}\exp\Big(-c'\widetilde{C}^{3/4}\Big).    
\end{equation}
In addition, by stationarity of the encoding one-sided infinite simple random walk $\mathcal{Z}$, for any positive integers $i<j$, the directed map $(\mathfrak{m}_{i,j},\vv{E}_{i,j})$ has the same law as the directed map $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,j-i},\vv{E}_{0,j-i})$. Thus for all positive integers $i<j$ and $\widetilde{C}>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:final bound stationary}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{i,j}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq \widetilde{C}(j-i)^{1/3}\Big]\leq c(j-i)^{3}\exp\Big(-c'\widetilde{C}^{3/4}\Big).    
\end{equation}
Now for any $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, the finite Mullin maps $\mathfrak{m}_{i,j},0\leq i\leq j\leq n,$ are all submaps of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$, and their associated orientations $\vv{E}_{i,j},0\leq i\leq j\leq n$ are all subsets of $\vv{E}_{0,n}$. As a result, it follows from \eqref{eq:final bound stationary} that for all $0\leq i<j\leq n$ and $\widetilde{C}>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:from i and j to n}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq \widetilde{C}n^{1/3}\Big]\leq \mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{i,j}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq \widetilde{C}(j-i)^{1/3}\Big]\leq cn^{3}\exp\Big(-c'\widetilde{C}^{3/4}\Big), \end{equation}
We then take $\widetilde{C}=C(\log n)^{2}$ in \eqref{eq:from i and j to n} and take a union bound on $0\leq i\leq j\leq n$ (note that $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})=0$ when $i=j$), which gives that for all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:union bound}
\begin{split}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]&\leq n^{2}\cdot cn^{3}\exp\Big(-c'C^{3/4}(\log n)^{3/2}\Big)\\
& = c\exp\Big(-c'C^{3/4}(\log n)^{3/2}+5\log n\Big).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
For all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$, the minimum of this last quantity and $1$ is uniformly bounded above by $c_{3} \exp(-c_{4} C^{3/4})$ for appropriate $c_{3},c_{4}>0$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:uniform upper bound ust}
There exist positive constants $c_{5}$ and $c_{6}$ such that for every $n\geq 2$, it holds for a directed UST map $(M_{n},\vv{E}_{M_{n}})$ with $n$ edges that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform upper bound ust}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{x,x'\in\mathcal{V}(M_{n})}\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(x,x')\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]\leq c_{5} \exp(-c_{6} C^{3/4})\qquad\text{for all $C>0$}.    
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Recall from below \Cref{def:Mullin map} that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, an ST map $(M,\vv{e},T)$ with $n$ edges can be viewed as a Mullin map of size $2n$. For every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, denote by $E_{n}$ the event that $\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}$ can be realized as an ST map with $n$ edges. Then by the Mullin bijection in \Cref{thm:Mullin bijection} and \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection}, for each $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, the probability of $E_{n}$ is equal to the number of ST maps with $n$ edges divided by $4^{2n}$. Now it is well-known (see e.g.~\cite[Equation (4.7)]{Mul67}) that the number of ST maps with $n$ edges is equal to 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:number of ST map}
\frac{(2n)!(2n+2)!}{n!\big[(n+1)!\big]^{2}(n+2)!}=\frac{1}{(n+1)^{2}}\cdot\frac{(2n)!}{(n!)^{2}}\cdot\frac{(2n+2)!}{n!(n+2)!}.    
\end{equation}
Combining this with Stirling's formula 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stirling formula}
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{k!}{k^{k}e^{-k}\sqrt{2\pi k}}=1,   
\end{equation}
we obtain that there exists a positive constant $\overline{c}$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prob of En}
\mathbb{P}[E_{n}] \geq \overline{c}n^{-3},\quad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.
\end{equation}

We have proved in \eqref{eq:union bound} that there exist positive constants $c,c'$ such that for any $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:union bound 2n}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]\leq c\exp\Big(-c'C^{3/4}(\log n)^{3/2}+5\log n\Big). 
\end{equation}
Now, for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, conditioned on the event $E_{n}$, the Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}$ can be realized as a uniform ST map with $n$ edges, which we denote by $(M_{n},\vv{e_{n}},T_{n})$ instead. Write $\vv{E}_{M_{n}}$ for the orientation associated with $(M_{n},\vv{e_{n}},T_{n})$ as explained in \Cref{def:Mullin map orientation}. Then it follows from \eqref{eq:prob of En} and \eqref{eq:union bound 2n} that there exist positive constants $c,c'$ such that for all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform bound ust 2n}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq Cn^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]\\
&=\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\mid E_{n}\Big]\\ 
&\leq \mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq C n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]/\mathbb{P}[E_{n}]\\
&\leq c\exp\Big(-c'C^{3/4}(\log n)^{3/2}+5\log n\Big)\cdot \overline{c}^{-1}n^{3}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Note that \eqref{eq:uniform bound ust 2n} only holds for $i\leq j$. To get around this, we use that by the correspondence between $(M_{n},\vv{e_{n}},T_{n})$ and the conditioned Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}_{0,2n}$ given below \Cref{def:Mullin map}, on the event $E_{n}$, both $u_{0}$ and $u_{2n}$ are equal to the terminal endpoint of $\vv{e_{n}}$. As a result, for all $0\leq i,j\leq 2n$, concatenating a directed path in $(M_{n},\vv{E}_{M_{n}})$ from $u_{i}$ to $u_{2n}$ and a directed path in $(M_{n},\vv{E}_{M_{n}})$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{j}$ gives a directed path in $(M_{n},\vv{E}_{M_{n}})$ from $u_{i}$ to $u_{j}$. So for all $0\leq i,j\leq 2n$, that $\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(u_{i},u_{j})$ is large implies that at least one of $\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(u_{i},u_{2n})$ and $\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(u_{0},u_{j})$ is large. Therefore, \eqref{eq:uniform bound ust 2n} implies that there exist positive constants $\widetilde{c},\widetilde{c}'$ such that for all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform bound ust all pair}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{x,x'\in\mathcal{V}(M_{n})}\mathrm{SDP}_{M_{n}}(x,x')\geq Cn^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}\Big]\leq \widetilde{c}\exp\Big(-\widetilde{c}'C^{3/4}(\log n)^{3/2}+8\log n\Big).
\end{equation}
For all $n\geq 2$ and $C>0$, the minimum of this last quantity and $1$ is uniformly bounded above by $c_{5} \exp(-c_{6} C^{3/4})$ for appropriate $c_{5},c_{6}>0$.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label[remark]{rem:uniform marked point}
The proofs of \Cref{thm:uniform upper bound Mullin map,thm:uniform upper bound ust} crucially use \Cref{lem:uniform marked point}, especially in \eqref{eq:prob of E}. We do not expect analogs of \Cref{lem:uniform marked point} to hold for models which are in the $\gamma$-LQG universality class for $\gamma\in(0,2)\setminus\{\sqrt{2}\}$. As a comment for LQG experts, this is related to the fact that the marked points on the left and right boundaries of a mated-CRT map cell are quantum typical points when $\gamma=\sqrt{2}$, but not for other values of $\gamma$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:constant upper and lower bound Mullin map}
For each $\delta\in(0,1)$, there exists $A=A(\delta)>1$ such that for each sufficiently large $n\geq N(\delta)$, the following holds with probability at least $1-\delta$ for the directed uniform Mullin map $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ of size n\footnote{Again, it follows from the Mullin bijection in \Cref{prop:Mullin bijection} that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ is a directed uniform Mullin map of size $n$.}:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*),leftmargin=*]
\item\label{constant bound:upper 1} For every vertex $u_{i}$ in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$, there exists a vertex $u_{j}$ in $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant upper bound 1}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\leq An^{1/3}.   \end{equation}
\item\label{constant bound:lower 2}
There exists a vertex $u_{i}$ in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant lower bound 2}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{i})\geq A^{-1}n^{1/3}. 
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, we fix the following convention. Suppose $\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}$ is a sequence of random variables with values in $\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ and $f(n)$ is a function from $\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}$ to $\mathbb{R}^{+}$. When we say that $X_{n}$ is stochastically bounded by $f(n)$, denoted by $X_{n}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\big(f(n)\big)$,
we mean that $X_{n}/f(n)$ is uniformly tight, that is, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whp at most}
\inf_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\big[X_{n}\leq Cf(n)\big]\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad C\to\infty.   \end{equation}

By the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$, for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $(\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty},\vv{E}_{n,\infty})$ has the same law as $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. So for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we can define the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}^{n}$ associated with $(\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty},\vv{E}_{n,\infty})$, which also has the same law as the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}$ associated with $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$. For the following proof, see \Cref{fig:constant upper bound} for an illustration.

\underline{\textbf{Bounds for $\mathcal{X}$ and $\mathcal{X}^{n}$.}} For a finite Mullin triangulation $\mathfrak{t}$, recall the definitions of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t},\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}$, $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}$ given above \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin triangulation}. Also recall that for $D\in \{\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t},\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t},\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}\}$, we use $\# D$ to denote the number of edges or half edges in $D$. For every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of sigmas}
\begin{split}
&\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}:=\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n},\quad \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}:=\#\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n},\quad\text{and}\\
&\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}:=\#\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n},\quad \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}:=\#\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}.    
\end{split}
\end{equation}

Then it follows from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin triangulation} that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigmas and walk}
\max\big\{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n},\underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n},\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n},\overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}\big\}\leq 2\max_{0\leq j\leq n}|\mathcal{Z}(j)|.    
\end{equation}
Because $\mathcal{Z}$ is a simple random walk on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$ with i.i.d.~increments that are mean $0$ and bounded in $[-1,1]$, applying Hoeffding's inequality yields that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whp uniform bound for sigma}
\max\big\{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n},\underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n},\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n},\overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}\big\} = O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n}).    
\end{equation}
Combined with \Cref{thm:scaling limit}, this further implies that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bounds for Bu and Bun}
\max\left\{\max_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}+1}|\mathcal{X}(j)|,\max_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}+1}|\mathcal{X}^{n}(j)|\right\} = O_{\mathbb{P}}\left((\sqrt{n})^{2/3}\right) = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3}).
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{Relating $\mathcal{X}$ and $\mathcal{X}^{n}$ to directed distances.}} Recall from above \Cref{thm:Busemann exists} the vertices $\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ with respect to which we define the Busemann function $\mathcal{X}$, and that for each $k\geq 0$, the vertex $x_{-k}$ (resp.\ $y_{k}$) is the $(k+1)$-th vertex on $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ (resp.\ $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$). Also recall from \Cref{def:infinite directed geodesic} and \Cref{cor:infinite directed geodesic} that for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, $P_{k}$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. For every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we use $\{x_{k}^{n}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ and $\{y_{k}^{n}\}_{k\geq 0}$ to denote the counterpart vertices of $\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ and $\{y_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{n,\infty}$, and use $\{P_{k}^{n}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ to denote the counterpart leftmost infinite directed geodesics of $\{P_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$ in  $(\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty},\vv{E}_{n,\infty})$. (The existence of these objects again follows from the stationarity of $\mathcal{Z}$.) 

For the remainder of this step, fix an arbitrary $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Then \eqref{eq:def of sigmas} implies that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x and y equal}
x_{-k-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}}=x_{-k-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}}^{n}\quad\text{and}\quad  y_{k+\underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}=y_{k+\overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}^{n},\quad\text{for every }k\geq 0,  
\end{equation}
which further implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x equal}
x_{-k-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}}=x_{-k-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}}^{n}\quad\text{for every }k\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad x_{k+\underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}=x_{k+\overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}^{n},\quad\text{for every }k\geq 1.
\end{equation}

Now we find a common coalescence point to compare the directed distances using \Cref{cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic}. Recall the definition, given above \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation}, of the $k$-th active edge $\lambda_k$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, whose endpoints are the red vertex $u_k$ and the blue vertex $v_k$. Define the times $T_{m},m\geq 0,$ as in \Cref{def:cone time}, which we know are almost surely all finite by \Cref{prop:infinitely many cone time}. We further know from \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex,def:cone time} and \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} that $u_{T_{m}},m\geq 0,$ are boundary cut vertices of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}$. Because $T_{m}\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$, there exists $m(n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ such that the vertices $\{x_{j}\}_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}$ and $\{x_{j}^{n}\}_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}}$ all lie in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,T_{m(n)}}$. Then $u_{T_{m(n)}}$ lies in $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}\cap\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{n,\infty}$ and $v_{T_{m(n)}}$ lies in $\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}\cap\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{n,\infty}$. Thus $u_{T_{m(n)}}$ is also a boundary cut vertex of $\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}$ by comparing to \Cref{def:boundary cut vertex}. \Cref{cor:two parts of leftmost infinite directed geodesic,cor:coalescence of leftmost infinite directed geodesic} then imply that $P_{j},-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}$ and $P_{j}^{n},-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}$ all need to coalesce at $u_{T_{m(n)}}$, and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp comparison}
\begin{split}
&\mathcal{X}(j)=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{j},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}}),\quad\text{for all }-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n};\\
&\mathcal{X}^{n}(j)=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{j}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}}),\quad\text{for all }-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1\leq j\leq \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{Bounding the difference of the normalizing terms.}} Recall the definition of $\tau_{k},k\geq 1$ in \eqref{eq:def of tauk and etak}. At time $n$, the point $x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}$ is not a vertex of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$, so we know $\tau_{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}+1}>n$ from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin triangulation}.
Then \Cref{lem:infinite directed geodesic contained in submap} implies that $P_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{\tau_{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}+1},\infty},\vv{E}_{\tau_{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}+1},\infty})$ from $x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}$ to $\infty$. Because $x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}=x_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}^{n}$ by \eqref{eq:x equal}, combining the uniqueness of leftmost infinite directed geodesic and the fact that $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}\supseteq\vv{E}_{n,\infty}\supseteq\vv{E}_{\tau_{\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}+1},\infty}$ then further implies  that $P_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}=P_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}^{n}$. This means
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp in 0 and n equal}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1},u_{T_{m(n)}})=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}}).    
\end{equation}
As a result, it follows from \eqref{eq:bounds for Bu and Bun}, \eqref{eq:sdp in 0 and n equal} and basic triangle inequalities that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp x0 and x0n}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ \Big|\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big|\\
&= \Big|\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big|\\
&\leq \Big|\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big|+\Big|\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big|\\
&=\big|-\mathcal{X}^{n}(-\overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1)\big|+\big|\mathcal{X}(-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}-1)\big|\\
&=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3})+O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}

\underline{\textbf{Conclusion.}} Consider an arbitrary vertex in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$ where $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. As $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}),n\geq 1,$ increase to $\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}$, this vertex must be some $x_{k}$ where $-\underline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}\leq k\leq \underline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}$. Because $P_{k}$ goes to $\infty$ and $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ is bounded, we can define $T=T(k,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ as the first time when the $T$-th directed edge $\vv{e}$ in $P_{k}$ does not belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$. Suppose $\vv{e}$ points from $u_{a_{T}}$ to $u_{b_{T}}$. Because $x_{k}$ lies in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ and the part of $P_{k}$ from $x_{k}$ to $u_{a_{T}}$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$, $u_{a_{T}}$ is a vertex in $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$. In addition, \Cref{lem:every geodesic into two parts} says that the part of $P_{k}$ from $u_{b_{T}}$ to $\infty$ is an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty},\vv{E}_{n,\infty})$. Now there are two cases for the directed edge $\vv{e}$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Case \Alph*}, leftmargin=1.5cm]
\item\label{case:red} The directed edge $\vv{e}$ is red. Recall from the end of \Cref{subsec:local limit} the definition of the time pair of a directed edge in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$. Then each red directed edge in $\vv{E}_{0,\infty}$ either belongs to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$ or belongs to $\vv{E}_{n,\infty}$, depending on whether its associated time pair $(b-1,b)$ satisfies $b\leq n$ or $b>n$. In light of this, since $\vv{e}$ is red and does not belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$, it has to belong to $\vv{E}_{n,\infty}$. So $u_{a_{T}}$ lies in both $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ and $\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}$. As a result, $u_{a_{T}}$ is a vertex $x_{-k^{n}}^{n}$ on $\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ for some $0\leq k^{n}\leq \overline{\sigma}_{L}^{n}$, and the part of $P_{k}$ after $u_{a_{T}}$ is exactly the same as $P^n_{-k^{n}}$ by uniqueness of leftmost infinite directed geodesic. See \Cref{fig:constant upper bound} left.
\item\label{case:black} The directed edge $\vv{e}$ is black. Then since $\vv{e}$ does not belong to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$, it has to cross a blue edge on $\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$. As a result, $u_{a_{T}}$ belongs to $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$, $u_{b_{T}}$ is equal to $x_{k^{n}}^{n}$ for some $1\leq k^{n}\leq \overline{\sigma}_{R}^{n}$, and the part of $P_{k}$ after $u_{b_{T}}$ is exactly the same as $P^n_{k^{n}}$ by uniqueness of leftmost infinite directed geodesic. See \Cref{fig:constant upper bound} right.
\end{enumerate}
For \ref{case:red}, it follows from \eqref{eq:sdp comparison} that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:case red}
\begin{split}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(x_{k},u_{a_{T}})&=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}\big(u_{a_{T}},u_{T_{m(n)}}\big) \\
& = \mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{-k^{n}}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\\
&= \Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
&\quad +\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
&\quad +\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{-k^{n}}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
& = \mathcal{X}(k)+\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)-\mathcal{X}^{n}(-k^{n}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
For \ref{case:black}, it again follows from \eqref{eq:sdp comparison} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:case black}
\begin{split}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(x_{k},u_{a_{T}})&=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-1-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}\big(u_{b_{T}},u_{T_{m(n)}}\big) \\
& = -1+\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{k^{n}}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\\
&= -1+\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{k},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
&\quad +\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
&\quad +\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{k^{n}}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)\\
& = -1+\mathcal{X}(k)+\Big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty}}(x_{0},u_{T_{m(n)}})-\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{n,\infty}}(x_{0}^{n},u_{T_{m(n)}})\Big)-\mathcal{X}^{n}(k^{n}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Plugging in \eqref{eq:bounds for Bu and Bun} and \eqref{eq:sdp x0 and x0n} yields that the right hand sides of \eqref{eq:case red} and \eqref{eq:case black} are both stochastically bounded by $n^{1/3}$. So using a union bound for the two cases, $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(x_{k},u_{a_{T}})$ is also stochastically bounded by $n^{1/3}$. Note that this bound holds for any $x_{k}$ in $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$. This is because the only place in the argument where we use an inequality related with the choice of $x_{k}$ is the last step, which applies \eqref{eq:bounds for Bu and Bun} and \eqref{eq:sdp x0 and x0n} that are both independent of the position of $x_{k}$. We thus complete the proof of \Cref{thm:constant upper and lower bound Mullin map}\ref{constant bound:upper 1}.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, we fix the following convention. Suppose $\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}$ is a sequence of random variables with values in $\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ and $g(n)$ is a function from $\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}$ to $\mathbb{R}$. When we say that with high probability $X_{n}$ is stochastically bounded away from $g(n)$, denoted by $X_{n} = \Omega_{\mathbb{P}}\big(g(n)\big)$,
we mean that (the added $1$ is to ensure strict positivity) $g(n)/(X_{n}+1)$ is uniformly tight, that is, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:whp at least}
\inf_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\big[X_{n}+1\geq cg(n)\big]\to 1\quad\text{as}\quad c\to 0.   
\end{equation}

\Cref{thm:scaling limit} says that as $k\to\infty$, the rescaled Busemann function $\big\{k^{-2/3}\mathcal{X}(-\lfloor kt\rfloor)\big\}_{t\geq 0}$ converges in law to a strictly stable mean zero $3/2$-stable L\'{e}vy process started from $0$ and with only upward jumps. Because such a L\'{e}vy process satisfies that $0$ is regular for $(-\infty,0)$, i.e.~it almost surely hits a negative number instantaneously, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:minimum bounded from below}
-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{X}(-j)=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{2/3}). 
\end{equation}

For a finite Mullin map $\mathfrak{t}$, recall the definitions of $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}$ given above \Cref{def:Mullin map to walk}. Also recall that for $D\in \{\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m},\underline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m},\overline{\partial}_{R}\mathfrak{m}\}$, we use $\# D$ to denote the number of edges or half edges in $D$. Then it follows from \Cref{lem:four boundary of Mullin map} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:length of pll ms}
\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}=-\min_{0\leq j\leq n}\mathcal{L}(j). 
\end{equation}
So we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigma L0 bounded from below}
\#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n}).    
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:minimum bounded from below} and \eqref{eq:sigma L0 bounded from below} then yields that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Busemann lower bound}
-\min_{0\leq j\leq \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}\mathcal{X}(-j)=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}}\big((\sqrt{n})^{2/3}\big) = \Omega_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3}).   
\end{equation}

Finally, recall from \Cref{def:infinite directed geodesic} and \Cref{cor:infinite directed geodesic} that for every $k\in\mathbb{Z}$, $P_{k}$ is the leftmost infinite directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{k}$ to $\infty$. Then for every $1\leq j\leq \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$, a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ from $x_{0}$ to $x_{-j}$ (which always exists since the red path along $\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ from $x_{0}$ to $x_{-j}$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$) followed by $P_{-j}$ forms an infinite directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$ from $x_{0}$ to $\infty$. As a result, we have the triangle inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:triangle equality lower}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(x_{0},x_{-j})\geq \mathcal{X}(0)-\mathcal{X}(-j)=-\mathcal{X}(-j),\quad\text{for every }1\leq j\leq \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}.    
\end{equation}
Combined with \eqref{eq:Busemann lower bound}, we obtain that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sdp lower bound}
\max_{0\leq j\leq \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(x_{0},x_{-j})=\Omega_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{1/3}).  
\end{equation}
The conclusion thus follows since the vertices $x_{-j},0\leq j\leq \#\underline{\partial}_{L}\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ all belong to $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label[proposition]{prop:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map}
For every $p\geq 0$, there exists a constant $c_{7}=c_{7}(p)$ such that with probability $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$ as $n\to\infty$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map}
\begin{split}
&\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(i^{*},j^{*})\geq \mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\quad\text{for all }1\leq i<j\leq n,\text{ and}\\
&\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(1^{*},n^{*})\leq c_{7}\log n\cdot\big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{n})+1\big).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{We first prove that the first inequality in \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map} holds almost}}\\ \underline{\textbf{surely for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and $1\leq i<j\leq n$.}} Fix $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Consider an arbitrary directed edge $\vv{e}^{*}$ in $\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n}$ from $a^{*}$ to $b^{*}$ where $1\leq a<b\leq n$. By definition, the triangles $\Delta_{a}$ and $\Delta_{b}$ share an edge $e$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ that crosses $\vv{e}^{*}$. There are three subcases.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin = *]
\item Suppose $e$ is red. Then the two endpoints of $e$ are respectively $u_{a}=u_{b-1}$ and $u_{b}=u_{a-1}$. In addition, since $1\leq a<b\leq n$, the directed edge associated with $e$ that points from $u_{a}$ to $u_{b}$ belongs to $\vv{E}_{0,n}$.
\item Suppose $e$ is blue. Then the unique red vertex of the blue triangle $\Delta_{a}$ (resp.\ $\Delta_{b}$) is $u_{a}$ (resp.\ $u_{b}$). In addition, since $1\leq a<b\leq n$, there is a black directed edge in $\vv{E}_{0,n}$ that crosses $e$ and points from $u_{a}$ to $u_{b}$.
\item Suppose $e$ is green. Then $a=b-1$. If $\mathcal{Z}(b)-\mathcal{Z}(b-1)$ is $(1,0)$ or $(-1,0)$, then there is a red directed edge in $\vv{E}_{0,n}$ that points from $u_{a}=u_{b-1}$ to $u_{b}$ since $1\leq a<b\leq n$; while if $\mathcal{Z}(b)-\mathcal{Z}(b-1)$ is $(0,1)$ or $(0,-1)$, then $u_{a}=u_{b-1}=u_{b}$.
\end{itemize}
In any case, we obtain that for any directed edge $\vv{e}^{*}$ in $\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n}$ from $a^{*}$ to $b^{*}$ where $1\leq a<b\leq n$, either $u_{a}=u_{b}$ or there is a directed edge in $\vv{E}_{0,n}$ from $u_{a}$ to $u_{b}$. Thus the first inequality in \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map} holds almost surely for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and $1\leq i<j\leq n$.

\underline{\textbf{We then consider the second inequality in \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map}.}} For every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, define the event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:good event}
E^{n}:=\{\text{every red vertex in }\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}\text{ has degree at most }C\log n\text{ in }\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}\}. 
\end{equation}
By \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20}, for every $p\geq 0$, there exists a constant $C=C(p)$ such that the probability of $E^{n}$, viewed as a function on $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, is equal to $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$\footnote{Here we explain \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20} in more detail using our terminology and notation. First, \cite{GHS20} considers the infinite Mullin triangulation $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$ constructed by applying the \emph{bi-infinite} version of the Mullin procedure in \Cref{def:Mullin procedure} to a bi-infinite simple random walk $\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}$ on $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$. Take $\mathfrak{t}_{-n,n}$ as the finite Mullin triangulation submap of $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$ corresponding to $\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|_{[-n,n]}$ via the Mullin bijection. The ``vertex of $M_{n}$" in \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20} means a red vertex in $\mathfrak{t}_{-n,n}$. In addition, \cite{GHS20} uses $\mathcal{T}$ to denote the dual cubic map of $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$, and views each triangle face of $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$ as a vertex in $\mathcal{T}$. In particular, the ``triangles of $\mathcal{T}$" in \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20} means triangle (vertices) of $\mathcal{T}$, or equivalently triangle (faces) of $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$. In all, \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20} says that for each $p>0$, there exists $C=C(p)>0$ such that the probability that each red vertex in $\mathfrak{t}_{-n,n}$ is incident to at most $C\log n$ triangles in $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$ is equal to $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$. Although we do not explain in detail about the bi-infinite version of the Mullin procedure in this work, \cite[Lemma 3.4]{GHS20} clearly fits our needs as $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ and $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ are respectively submaps of $\mathfrak{t}_{-n,n}$ and $\mathfrak{t}_{-\infty,\infty}$.}. We then bound $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(1^{*},n^{*})$ from above in terms of $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{n})$ on the event $E^{n}$, which will yield the conclusion.

Fix $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$. Recall above \Cref{lem:aj bj are increasing} the definitions of the vertex sets $\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m})$ and $\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m})$ associated with a finite Mullin map $\mathfrak{m}$, and the definition of the time pair of a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{m},\vv{E}_{\mathfrak{m}})$ (see also the end of \Cref{subsec:local limit} for the same definition regarding directed edges in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}_{0,\infty})$). Now suppose $P$ is a directed geodesic in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{n}$, which always exists since the clockwise boundary arc of $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$ from $u_{0}$ to $u_{n}$ is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$. Write $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{n})=k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ so that $P$ has length $k$. For every $1\leq j\leq k$, let $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ be the $j$-th directed edge on $P$ where $(a_{j},b_{j})$ is its time pair. Set $b_{0}=0$ and $a_{k+1}=n$. Because $u_{0}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$, $u_{n}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n})$, and a directed geodesic is always simple, 
it follows from applying \Cref{lem:aj bj are increasing} to $P$ and $(\mathfrak{m}_{0,n},\vv{E}_{0,n})$ that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:increasing sequence proof}
0=b_{0}\leq a_{1}< b_{1}\leq a_{2}< b_{2}\leq\cdots\leq a_{j}< b_{j}\leq \cdots \leq a_{k}< b_{k}\leq a_{k+1}=n.    
\end{equation}
We then prove the following two results.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth]{Figures/first_step_of_comparison.png}
\caption{Illustration for the proof of the second inequality in \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map}. We color the contour exploration of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ in gray, and dash the edges in $\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,\infty}$. \textbf{Left:} Illustration for the proof of \ref{property:A}. The black directed path shows the directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\protect\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ that visits $\Delta_{N_{\ell}},1\leq \ell\leq m(j)$ in order. \textbf{Right:} Illustration for the conclusion using \ref{property:B} and \ref{property:A} alternatively. The directed geodesic $P$ is colored in orange, and the black directed path is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\protect\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$.}
\label{fig:first step of comparison}
\vspace{0.5cm}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Result \Alph*}, leftmargin=2cm]
\item\label{property:A} For every $0\leq j\leq k$, $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j}+1}$. In this case, we show that on the event $E^{n}$, when $1\leq j\leq k$, there is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $b_{j}^{*}$ to $a_{j+1}^{*}$ with length at most $C\log n$. The same argument will also show that on the event $E^{n}$, when $j=0$ and $a_{1}>0$, there is a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $1^{*}$ to $a_{1}^{*}$ with length at most $C\log n$.
\item\label{property:B} For every $1\leq j\leq k$, $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ points from $u_{a_{j}}$ to $u_{b_{j}}$. In this case, we show that when $a_{j}>0$, there is a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $a_{j}^{*}$ to $b_{j}^{*}$; while when $a_{j}=0$ we must have $j=1$ and $b_{1}=1$.
\end{enumerate}

\underline{\textbf{We first prove \ref{property:A}.}} Fix $0\leq j\leq k$. We enumerate all the red or blue triangles incident to the vertex $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$ in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ as $\{\Delta_{N_{\ell}}\}_{1\leq \ell\leq m(j)}$ where $m(j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ and $N_{\ell},1\leq \ell\leq m(j)$ are increasing. Then $m(j)\leq C\log n$ by the definition of $E^{n}$ in \eqref{eq:good event}. In addition, we claim that for each $1\leq \ell< m(j)$, the two triangles $\Delta_{N_{\ell}}$ and $\Delta_{N_{\ell+1}}$ are neighboring. See \Cref{fig:first step of comparison} left for an illustration. Fix $1\leq \ell<m
(j)$. If $N_{\ell+1}=N_{\ell}+1$, then $\Delta_{N_{\ell}}$ and $\Delta_{N_{\ell+1}}$ share a green edge. If $N_{\ell+1}>N_{\ell}+1$, then because $\Delta_{N_{\ell}+1}$ is incident to $u_{N_{\ell}}$, the vertex $u_{N_{\ell}}$ is different from $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$. However, $\Delta_{N_{\ell}}$ is both incident to $u_{N_{\ell}}$ and $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, so it must be a red triangle corresponding to a step $(1,0)$ or $(-1,0)$ of $\mathcal{Z}$. Consider these two possible cases of $\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell}-1)$ and the corresponding steps of Mullin procedure that builds $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
\item Suppose $\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell}-1)=(1,0)$. Then since $u_{N_{\ell}}$ is different from $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, $\Delta_{r},N_{\ell}<r<N_{\ell+1}$ will not be incident to the vertex $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j}+1}$ until $\Delta_{N_{\ell+1}}$, which corresponds to a $(-1,0)$ step of $\mathcal{Z}$ and shares a red edge with $\Delta_{N_{\ell}}$.
\item Suppose $\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell})-\mathcal{Z}(N_{\ell}-1)=(-1,0)$. Then since $u_{N_{\ell}}$ is different from $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, $\Delta_{r},r>N_{\ell}$ can never be incident to the vertex $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j}+1}$, a contradiction to the assumption $\ell<m(j)$.
\end{itemize}
In any case, for every $1\leq \ell<m(j)$, because $\Delta_{N_{\ell}}$ and $\Delta_{N_{\ell+1}}$ are neighbouring, there is a directed edge $\vv{e_{\ell}}$ in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,\infty})$ that points from $N_{\ell}^{*}$ to $N_{\ell+1}^{*}$. Now if $1\leq j\leq k$, then $1\leq b_{j}\leq a_{j+1}\leq n$ by \eqref{eq:increasing sequence proof}. As the triangles $\Delta_{b_{j}},\Delta_{a_{j+1}}$ are both incident to the vertex $u_{b_{j}}=u_{a_{j+1}}$, there exist $1\leq \ell_{1}\leq \ell_{2}\leq m(j)$ such that $b_{j}=N_{\ell_{1}}$ and $a_{j+1}=N_{\ell_{2}}$. Concatenating $\vv{e_{\ell}},\ell_{1}\leq \ell<\ell_{2}$ then gives a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $b_{j}^{*}$ to $a_{j+1}^{*}$, which has length at most $C\log n$ since $m(j)\leq C \log n$. While if $j=0$ and $a_{1}>0$, then $N_{1}=1$. As the triangle $\Delta_{a_{1}}$ is incident to the vertex $u_{0}=u_{a_{1}}$, there exists $1\leq \ell_{2}\leq m(0)$ such that $a_{1}=N_{\ell_{2}}$. Concatenating $\vv{e_{\ell}},1\leq \ell<\ell_{2}$ then gives a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $1^{*}$ to $a_{1}^{*}$, which has length at most $C\log n$ since $m(0)\leq C \log n$. So we have proved \ref{property:A}.

\underline{\textbf{We then prove \ref{property:B}.}} Fix $1\leq j\leq k$. Recall the discussions regarding a time pair above \Cref{lem:aj bj are increasing}. If the edge $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ that points from $u_{a_{j}}$ to $u_{b_{j}}$ is red, then $a_{j}=b_{j}-1$; while if $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$ is black, then $1\leq a_{j}< b_{j}$, and the triangles $\Delta_{a_{j}},\Delta_{b_{j}}$ share the unique blue edge in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$ crossed by $\vv{e}_{(a_{j},b_{j})}$. So $a_{j}=0$ implies $j=1$ and $b_{j}=1$. In addition, if $a_{j}>0$, then $\Delta_{a_{j}}$ and $\Delta_{b_{j}}$ are neighbouring, and there is a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,\infty})$ that points from $a_{j}^{*}$ to $b_{j}^{*}$. This directed edge has to lie in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ as $0< a_{j}\leq b_{j}\leq n$ by \eqref{eq:increasing sequence proof}. So we have proved \ref{property:B}.

\underline{\textbf{Conclusion.}} Finally, assume that $E^{n}$ holds. If $a_{1}=0$, then $b_{1}=1$ by \ref{property:B}, and we first use \ref{property:A} for $j=1$, then alternatively use \ref{property:B} and \ref{property:A} for $2\leq j\leq k$. While if $a_{1}>0$, then we first use \ref{property:A} for $j=0$, then alternatively use \ref{property:B} and \ref{property:A} for $1\leq j\leq k$. In any case, we are able to find a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ that (1) goes through $b_{j}^{*},a_{j+1}^{*},1\leq j\leq k$ in the order of \eqref{eq:increasing sequence proof}; (2) starts at $1^{*}$ and ends at $n^{*}$; and (3) has length no more than $C\log n(k+1)+k$. See \Cref{fig:first step of comparison} right for an illustration. So the second inequality of \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map} holds on the event $E^{n}$ when we take $c_{7}=2C$ and assume $n\geq 10$. The conclusion thus follows.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label[proposition]{prop:comparison cubic map and other model}
There is a sequence of couplings labeled by $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, where the $n$-th coupling is a coupling of  $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]},\mathfrak{Z}|_{[0,n]},Z|_{[0,n]}$, such that the following holds. For each $p>0$, there is a constant $c_{8}=c_{8}(p)>0$ such that under the coupling of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]},\mathfrak{Z}|_{[0,n]},Z|_{[0,n]}$, with probability $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$ as $n\to\infty$, we have that for all $1\leq i<j\leq n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison cubic map and other model}
\begin{split}
&c_{8}^{-1}(\log n)^{-3}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(i^{*},j^{*})\leq \mathrm{SDP}_{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,n}}(i,j)\leq c_{8}(\log n)^{3}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(i^{*},j^{*}),\quad\text{and}\\
&c_{8}^{-1}(\log n)^{-3}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(i^{*},j^{*})\leq \mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(i,j)\leq c_{8}(\log n)^{3}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}(i^{*},j^{*}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
\underline{\textbf{Coupling and setup.}} Clearly $\sqrt{2}\mathcal{Z}$ and $\mathfrak{Z}$ both have i.i.d.~increments of mean zero and identity covariance matrix. By the multi-dimensional strong coupling theorem \cite[Theorem 1.3]{Zai98} (the higher dimensional analogue of \cite{KMT76}), there exist positive constants $b_{0}$ and $b_{1}$ that only depend on the law of $\sqrt{2}\mathcal{Z}$ and $\mathfrak{Z}$ such that the following holds. One can construct a sequence of couplings labeled by $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, where the $n$-th coupling is a coupling of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]},\mathfrak{Z}|
_{[0,n]},Z|_{[0,n]}$ whose corresponding probability measure (resp.\ expectation) is denoted by $\mathbb{P}_{n}$ (resp.\ $\mathbb{E}^{n}$), such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:KMT coupling}
\mathbb{E}^{n}\Big[\exp\Big(b_{0}\max_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}}\big|\sqrt{2}\mathcal{Z}(j)-Z(j)\big|\Big)\Big]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}^{n}\Big[\exp\Big(b_{0}\max_{j\in[0,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}}\big|\mathfrak{Z}(j)-Z(j)\big|\Big)\Big], 
\end{equation}
both viewed as functions on $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, are both equal to $O_{n}(n^{b_{1}})$. We now prove that this sequence of couplings labeled by $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$ satisfies the requirement in the statement.

For every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, we now construct an event $E^{n}=E^{n}(C_{0})$ that depends on $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]},\mathfrak{Z}|_{[0,n]},Z|_{[0,n]}$ and a positive constant $C_{0}$ which does not depend on $n$. Later on, we will show that (1) for every $p>0$, one can choose $C_{0}=C_{0}(p)$ such that $\mathbb{P}_{n}[E^{n}]=1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$; and (2) for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model} holds on the event $E^{n}$ with $c_{8}=C_{0}^{3}$, which will conclude the proof. The three conditions for the event $E^{n}$ are
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*),leftmargin=*]
\item\label{condition:little difference at integer points} We have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:little difference at integer points}
\max_{j\in[0,n]\cap{Z}}\big|\sqrt{2}\mathcal{Z}(j)-Z(j)\big|\leq \frac{C_{0}}{10}\log n\quad\text{and}\quad \max_{j\in[0,n]\cap{Z}}\big|\mathfrak{Z}(j)-Z(j)\big|\leq \frac{C_{0}}{10}\log n.
\end{equation}
\item\label{condition:Brownian motion little fluctuation} We have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Brownian motion little fluctuation}
\sup_{s,t\in[i-1,i]}\big|Z(s)-Z(t)\big|\leq \log n,\quad\text{for each }i\in[1,n]\cap\mathbb{Z}.  \end{equation}
\item\label{condition:close point upper bound} For each pair of integers $(i_{1},i_{2})$ with $0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq n$ and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Brownian motion close}
\inf_{t\in[i_{1},i_{2}]}\big(L(t)-L(i_{1})\big)\geq -6C_{0}\log n \quad\text{and}\quad \big|L(i_{2})-L(i_{1})\big|\leq 6C_{0}\log n,    
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:close point upper bound}
\#\Big\{j\in[i_{1},i_{2}]\cap\mathbb{Z}:\inf_{t\in[j-1,j]}\big(L(t)-L(i_{1})\big)\leq 7C_{0}\log n\Big\}
\leq C_{0}^{3}(\log n)^{3}.    
\end{equation}
Furthermore, the same holds with $R$ in place of $L$.
\end{enumerate}
We remark that our definition of the event $E^{n}=E^{n}(C_{0})$ is the same as that of $E^{n}=E^{n}(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})$ in \cite{GHS20} (see above Lemma 2.2 of the referenced paper), except that the choices of constants are slightly changed and that the interval $[-n,n]$ in \cite{GHS20} is changed into $[0,n]$ here. Because these changes are inconsequential, the following counterpart of \cite[Lemma 2.2]{GHS20} follows from the same proof of the referenced result.
\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Lemma 2.2]{GHS20}}]\label[lemma]{lem:En on walks with high probability}
For each $p>0$, if the constant $C_{0}$ is chosen sufficiently large in a manner depending only on $p$, then $\mathbb{P}_{n}[E^{n}]=1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$.    
\end{lemma}

Choose $C_{0}=C_{0}(p)$ large enough as in the statement of \Cref{lem:En on walks with high probability}. We then prove that on the event $E^{n}$, \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model} holds with $c_{8}=C_{0}^{3}$. Assume $E^{n}$ in the remainder of this proof.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{Figures/strong_KMT_coupling.png}
\caption{An illustration for the proof of \Cref{prop:comparison cubic map and other model}. We take case \ref{comparison:lower 1} as an example. The red line indicates \eqref{eq:meandric system connection up}, the green region indicates a band of width $3C_
{0}\log n$, and the blue dots indicate points in $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$. The figure does not completely align with the choice of constants in the definition of $E^{n}$.}
\label{fig:KMT strong coupling}
\vspace{0.5cm}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{comparison:lower 1}\underline{\textbf{Bound $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}$ from above in terms of $\mathrm{SDP}_{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,n}}$.}} For any $1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq n$ such that there is a directed edge in $(\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}}_{0,n})$ that points from the point $i_{1}$ to the point $i_{2}$, we construct a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ with length at most $C_{0}^{3}(\log n)^{3}$ that goes from $i_{1}^{*}$ to $i_{2}^{*}$. This will suffice to give the first lower bound in \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model}. As $\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n}$ contains a directed edge from $i^{*}$ to $(i+1)^{*}$ for all $1\leq i<n$, the case $i_{1}=i_{2}-1$ is trivial. Fix $i_{1}$ and $i_{2}$ such that $i_{1}<i_{2}-1$. 

By \eqref{eq:infinite meandric system connection}, the condition that there is a directed edge in $\vv{E}^{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}}_{0,n}$ from $i_{1}$ to $i_{2}$ implies that either
\begin{equation}\label{eq:meandric system connection up}
\mathfrak{L}(i_{1}-1)=\mathfrak{L}(i_{2})<\min_{k\in[i_{1},i_{2}-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{L}(k),    
\end{equation}
or the same holds with $\mathfrak{R}$ in place of $\mathfrak{L}$. Assume without loss of generality that we are in the former setting. Let us consider the set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of Fmpwl}
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}:=\Big\{k\in[i_{1},i_{2}]\cap\mathbb{Z}:\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k-1)\wedge\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k)-\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{1})\leq 3C_{0}\log n\Big\}.  
\end{equation}
See \Cref{fig:KMT strong coupling} for an illustration. First, combining \eqref{eq:meandric system connection up} with condition \ref{condition:little difference at integer points} in the definition of $E^{n}$ and using the triangle inequality, we know that both $i_{1}$ and $i_{2}$ are contained in $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$. Second, combining \eqref{eq:meandric system connection up} with conditions \ref{condition:little difference at integer points} and \ref{condition:Brownian motion little fluctuation} in the definition of $E^{n}$, it follows that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Brownian motion not large}
\inf_{t\in[i_{1},i_{2}]}\big(L(t)-L(i_{1})\big)\geq -3C_{0}\log n\quad\text{and}\quad\big|L(i_{2})-L(i_{1})\big|\leq 3C_{0}\log n.   
\end{equation}
In addition, combining the definition of $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$ in \eqref{eq:def of Fmpwl} with conditions \ref{condition:little difference at integer points} and \ref{condition:Brownian motion little fluctuation} in the definition of $E^{n}$ yields that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Fmpwl is contained}
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq\Big\{k\in[i_{1},i_{2}]\cap\mathbb{Z}:L(k)-L(i_{1})\leq 7C_{0}\log n\Big\}.    
\end{equation}
With \eqref{eq:Brownian motion not large} and \eqref{eq:Fmpwl is contained} in hand, the cardinality of $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$ is then at most $C_{0}^{3}(\log n)^{3}$ by condition \ref{condition:close point upper bound} in  the definition of $E^{n}$. Third, if $k_{1}<k_{2}$ are consecutive numbers in $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$, then $1\leq i_{1}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}\leq i_{2}\leq n$,    
and either $k_{1}=k_{2}-1$ or 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:consecutive points meaning}
\begin{split}
&\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k_{1}-1)-\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{1})\leq 3C_{0}\log n,\quad  \sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k_{2})-\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{1})\leq 3C_{0}\log n,\\
&\text{and}\quad \min_{k\in[k_{1},k_{2}-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}}\big(\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k) - \sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{1})\big)>3C_{0}\log n.     
\end{split}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:discrete mated map connection}, in both cases there is a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $k_{1}^{*}$ to $k_{2}^{*}$. The construction of the directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ from $i_{1}^{*}$ to $i_{2}^{*}$ then follows from combining the last three properties regarding $\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{L}}$.

\item\label{comparison:upper 1}\underline{\textbf{Bound $\mathrm{SDP}_{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,n}}$ from above in terms of $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}$.}} The first upper bound in \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model} follows from the verbatim argument in \ref{comparison:lower 1} after swapping $\sqrt{2}\mathcal{Z}$ with $\mathfrak{Z}$ and swapping $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ with $(\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}}_{0,n})$.

\item\label{comparison:lower 2}\underline{\textbf{Bound $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}$ from above in terms of $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}$.}} For any $1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq n$ such that there is a directed edge in $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$ that points from the point $i_{1}$ to the point $i_{2}$, we construct a directed path in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ with length at most $C_{0}^{3}(\log n)^{3}$ that goes from $i_{1}^{*}$ to $i_{2}^{*}$. This will suffice to prove the second lower bound in \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model}. By \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection}, the condition that there is a directed edge in $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$ from $i_{1}$ to $i_{2}$ implies that either
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mated-CRT up}
\left(\inf_{t\in[i_{1}-1,i_{1}]}L(t)\right)\vee\left(\inf_{t\in[i_{2}-1,i_{2}]}L(t)\right)\leq \inf_{t\in[i_{1},i_{2}-1]}L(t),   
\end{equation}
or the same holds with $R$ in place of $L$. (Note that \eqref{eq:mated-CRT up} always holds when $i_{1}=i_{2}-1$.) The remaining proof of \ref{constant bound:upper 1} after \eqref{eq:meandric system connection up} then work verbatim after replacing \eqref{eq:meandric system connection up} by \eqref{eq:mated-CRT up}.

\item\label{comparison:upper 2}\underline{\textbf{Bound $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}$ from above in terms of $\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n}}$.}} For any $1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq n$ such that there is a directed edge in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n})$ that points from $i_{1}^{*}$ to $i_{2}^{*}$, we construct a directed path in $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$ with length at most $C_{0}^{3}(\log n)^{3}$ that goes from the point $i_{1}$ to the point $i_{2}$. This will suffice to prove the second upper bound in \eqref{eq:comparison cubic map and other model}. As $\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n}$ contains a directed edge from $i$ to $i+1$ for all $1\leq i<n$, the case $i_{1}=i_{2}-1$ is trivial. Fix $i_{1}$ and $i_{2}$ such that $i_{1}<i_{2}-1$. 

By \eqref{eq:discrete mated map connection} the condition that there is a directed edge in $\vv{E}^{*}_{0,n}$ from $i_{1}^{*}$ to $i_{2}^{*}$ implies that either 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete mated map connection up}
\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{1}-1)=\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(i_{2})<\min_{i_{1}\leq k\leq i_{2}-1}\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}(k),  \end{equation}
or the same holds with $\sqrt{2}\mathcal{R}$ in place of $\sqrt{2}\mathcal{L}$. Assume without loss of generality that we are in the former setting. Let us consider the set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of Fbsl}
\mathcal{V}_{L}:=\Big\{k\in[i_{1},i_{2}]\cap\mathbb{Z}:\inf_{t\in[k-1,k]}\big(L(t)-L(i_{1})\big)\leq 3C_{0}\log n\Big\}.  
\end{equation}
First, combining \eqref{eq:discrete mated map connection up} and conditions \ref{condition:little difference at integer points} and \ref{condition:Brownian motion little fluctuation} in the definition of $E^{n}$, we know that \eqref{eq:Brownian motion not large} also holds here. As a result, both $i_{1}$ and $i_{2}$ are contained in $\mathcal{V}_{L}$. Second, \eqref{eq:Brownian motion not large} also implies that the cardinality of $\mathcal{V}_{L}$ is at most $C_{0}^3(\log n)^{3}$ by condition \ref{condition:close point upper bound} in the definition of $E^{n}$. Third, if $k_{1}<k_{2}$ are consecutive numbers in $\mathcal{V}_{L}$, then $1\leq i_{1}\leq k_{1}<k_{2}\leq i_{2}\leq n$,    
and there is a directed edge in $\mathcal{G}_{0,n}$ from $k_{1}$ to $k_{2}$ by \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection}. The construction of the directed path in $\mathcal{G}_{0,n}$ then follows from combining the last three properties regarding $\mathcal{V}_{L}$.\qedhere 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label[lemma]{lem:En on walks with high probability}
For each $p>0$, if the constant $C_{0}$ is chosen sufficiently large in a manner depending only on $p$, then $\mathbb{P}_{n}[E^{n}]=1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Recall above \Cref{lem:boundary of infinite Mullin triangulation} the definition of the $n$-th active edge $\lambda_{n}$ of $\mathfrak{t}_{0,\infty}$, whose endpoints are the red vertex $u_{n}$ and the blue vertex $v_{n}$. In particular, for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, $u_{i},0\leq i\leq n$ are all the red vertices in $\mathfrak{t}_{0,n}$ and $\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,label=(\arabic*)]
\item\label{proof:upper mated-CRT}\underline{\textbf{The upper bound in \Cref{thm:bound mated crt} regarding $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$.}} Applying \Cref{thm:uniform upper bound Mullin map} with $C=(\log n)^{5/3}$ implies that for each $p>0$, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform upper bound Mullin map superpolynomial decay}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{n})\geq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{11/3}\Big]=O_{n}(n^{-p}).    
\end{equation}
In addition, combining \Cref{prop:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map,prop:comparison cubic map and other model} further implies that there is a sequence of couplings labeled by $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, where the $n$-th coupling is a coupling on  $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}$ and $Z|_{[0,n]}$, such that the following holds. For each $p>0$, there exists a constant $C=C(p)$ such that under the coupling of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}$ and $Z|_{[0,n]}$, with probability $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$ as $n\to\infty$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison Mullin cell and other model upper}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(1,n)\leq C(\log n)^{4}\big(\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{0},u_{n})+1\big).
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:uniform upper bound Mullin map superpolynomial decay} and \eqref{eq:comparison Mullin cell and other model upper} then yields that for each $p>0$, we have (here we use $8>4+11/3$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal upper bound start to end}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(1,n)\geq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{8}\Big]=O_{n}(n^{-p}).
\end{equation}
Now, recall that for all integers $i<j$, the directed planar map $(\mathcal{G}_{i,j},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i,j})$ is determined by $Z|_{[i,j]}$ in the manner of \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection} that only depends on $j-i$ and $Z(t)-Z(i),i\leq t\leq j$. So it follows from \eqref{eq:universal upper bound start to end}, the stationarity of $Z$ and a union bound that for each $p>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal upper bound all point}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{0\leq i<j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{i,j}}(i+1,j) \geq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{8}\Big] = O_{n}(n^{-p}).
\end{equation}
Note that every directed path in $(\mathcal{G}_{0,\infty},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,\infty})$ can only go in the increasing order of points on $\mathbb{Z}$, and that for all $0\leq i<j\leq n$, the orientation $\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i,j}$ already includes all the directed edges in $\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,\infty}$ with both endpoints in $[i+1,j]\cap\mathbb{Z}$. As a result, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:directed length are equal mated-CRT}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{i,j}}(i+1,j)=\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(i+1,j),\quad\text{for all }0\leq i<j\leq n.    
\end{equation}
Combining this with \eqref{eq:universal upper bound all point} then gives the upper bound in \eqref{eq:constant upper and lower bound mated crt}.

\item\label{proof:lower mated-CRT}\underline{\textbf{The lower bound in \Cref{thm:bound mated crt} regarding $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$.}} \Cref{thm:constant upper and lower bound Mullin map} implies that for each $\delta>0$, there exists $A=A(\delta)>1$ such that for each sufficiently large $n\geq N(\delta)$, it happens with at least $1-\delta$ probability that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant lower bound Mullin map polylog}
\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j})\geq A^{-1}n^{1/3}-1.    
\end{equation}
In addition, combining \Cref{prop:comparison Mullin cell and cubic map,prop:comparison cubic map and other model} further implies that there is a sequence of couplings labeled by $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, where the $n$-th coupling is a coupling on  $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}$ and $Z|_{[0,n]}$, such that the following holds. For each $p>0$, there exists a constant $C=C(p)$ such that under the coupling of $\mathcal{Z}|_{[0,n]}$ and $Z|_{[0,n]}$, with probability $1-O_{n}(n^{-p})$ as $n\to\infty$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison Mullin cell and other model lower}
\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(i,j)\geq C^{-1}(\log n)^{-4}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathfrak{m}_{0,n}}(u_{i},u_{j}),\quad\text{for all }1\leq i<j\leq n.  
\end{equation}
Combining this with \eqref{eq:constant lower bound Mullin map polylog} then gives that (here we use $17/4>4$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lower bound constant error}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,n}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-17/4}\Big] = 0.    
\end{equation}
Now, for each $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, write $K_{n}:=[n/(\log n)^{2}]$. Applying \eqref{eq:lower bound constant error} with $n=K_{n}$ then implies that (here we use $5>17/4+2/3$)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lower bound constant error Kn}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq K_{n}}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{0,K_{n}}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-5}\Big] = 0.    
\end{equation}
Recall that for all integers $i<j$, the directed planar map $(\mathcal{G}_{i,j},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{i,j})$ is determined by $Z|_{[i,j]}$ in the manner of \eqref{eq:mated-CRT connection} that only depends on $j-i$ and $Z(t)-Z(i),i\leq t\leq j$. So it follows from \eqref{eq:lower bound constant error Kn} and the independence and stationarity of $Z$ that for each $p>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bundles of length Kn}
\begin{split}
&\quad\ \mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{(a-1)K_{n}+1\leq i\leq j\leq aK_{n}}\mathrm{SDP}_{\mathcal{G}_{(a-1)K_{n},aK_{n}}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-5},\forall 1\leq a\leq [(\log n)^{2}]\Big]\\  
&=O_{n}\Big((1/2)^{(\log n)^{2}}\Big)=O_{n}(n^{-p}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Combining this with \eqref{eq:directed length are equal mated-CRT} then gives the lower bound in \eqref{eq:constant upper and lower bound mated crt}.


\item\label{proof:upper ms}\underline{\textbf{Proof of \Cref{thm:bound ms} regarding $(\mathit{MS}_{n},\vv{E}^{\mathit{MS}}_{n})$.}} After replacing $n$ with $2n$, replacing $Z$ with $\mathfrak{Z}$, and replacing $(\mathcal{G}_{0,n},\vv{E}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0,n})$ with $(\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,2n},\vv{E}^{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}}_{0,2n})$, the verbatim proof in \ref{proof:upper mated-CRT} gives that for each $p>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:upper bound start to end universal ims}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,2n}}(i,j)\geq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{8}\Big] = O_{n}(n^{-p}), 
\end{equation}
and the verbatim proof in \ref{proof:lower mated-CRT} gives that for each $p>0$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lower bound start to end universal ims}
\mathbb{P}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq j\leq 2n}\mathrm{SDP}_{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,2n}}(i,j)\leq n^{1/3}(\log n)^{-5}\Big] = O_{n}(n^{-p}). 
\end{equation}
Consider the pair of independent one-dimensional simple random walk $\mathfrak{Z}=(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{R})$ that is equal to $(0,0)$ at time $0$. If we condition on the event that both $\mathfrak{L}|_{[0,2n]}$ and $\mathfrak{R}|_{[0,2n]}$ are Dyck paths of length $2n$, that is, they both end at $0$ and always stay non-negative, then the conditional law of $(\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}_{0,2n},\vv{E}^{\widetilde{\mathit{MS}}}_{0,2n})$ is the same as that of the directed uniform meandric system $(\mathit{MS}_{n},\vv{E}^{\mathit{MS}}_{n})$ of size $n$. Since the number of Dyck paths of length $2n$ is equal to $\binom{2n}{n}/(n+1)$, it follows from Stirling's formula \eqref{eq:stirling formula} that there is an absolute constant $\overline{c}>0$ such that for every $n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}$, the probability of the above event that we are conditioning on is at least $\overline{c}n^{-3}$. Combining this with \eqref{eq:upper bound start to end universal ims} and \eqref{eq:lower bound start to end universal ims} then respectively gives the upper and lower bound in \eqref{eq:constant upper and lower bound ms}.\qedhere
\end{enumerate}
